Rogue waves and large deviations for 2D pure gravity deep water waves
M. Berti, R. Grande, A. Maspero, G. Staffilani

Abstract
Rogue waves are extreme ocean events characterized by the sudden formation of anomalously large crests, and remain an important subject of investigation in oceanography and mathematics. A central problem is to quantify the probability of their formation under random Gaussian sea initial data. In this work, we rigorously characterize the tail-probability for the formation of rogue waves of the pure gravity water wave equations in deep water, the most accurate quasilinear PDE modeling waves in open ocean. This large deviation result rigorously proves various conjectures from the oceanography literature in the weakly nonlinear regime. Moreover, the result holds up to the optimal timescales allowed by deterministic well-posedness theory. The proof shows that rogue waves most likely arise through “dispersive focusing”, where phase quasi-synchronization produces constructive amplification of the water crest. The main difficulty in justifying this mechanism is propagating statistical information over such long timescales, which we overcome by combining normal forms and probabilistic methods. Unlike prior work, this novel approach does not require approximate solutions to be Gaussian. Our general method tracks the tail probability of solutions to Hamiltonian PDEs with an integrable normal form and random Gaussian initial data over very long times, even in the absence of (quasi-)invariant measures.



1
Introduction

Rogue waves are extreme ocean phenomena characterized by waves of anomalous height.
They are nowadays recognized as serious threats to ships and sea structures: it is estimated that, between 1969 and 1994, 22 super-carriers were lost and 525 fatalities occurred due to collisions with rogue waves in the Pacific and Atlantic Oceans
[
49
]
.
The first rogue wave ever recorded was the Draupner wave, which reached a height of 25.6 meters when the significant wave height –defined as four times the standard deviation of the surface elevation– was at most 12 meters
[
31
]
. This event illustrates the textbook definition of a rogue wave: a water wave whose crest-to-trough height exceeds twice the significant wave height.
In the past decade, datasets of measured rogue waves have become available, see e.g.
[
18
,
2
]
, providing valuable information for statistical analysis.

Explaining the formation of rogue waves remains a challenging and debated problem in oceanography and physics, see e.g.
[
32
,
58
]
.
Open questions include whether rogue waves can be forecast
[
42
,
13
,
36
]
and whether certain sea conditions make their occurrence more likely, see for instance
[
65
]
.
Two prominent theories proposed to explain the generation of rogue waves are
nonlinear focusing
and
dispersive focusing
.

According to the nonlinear focusing theory, rogue waves form as a late stage of modulational instability, see, for example,
[
57
,
34
,
73
]
for theoretical analyses and
[
16
,
17
]
for experimental evidence in water tanks. Theoretically, this mechanism is described by the one-dimensional focusing semilinear Schrödinger equation (NLS), which is a modulational asymptotic model for pure gravity deep water waves
[
71
,
66
]
. The NLS equation
possesses explicit families of solutions that exhibit rogue-wave-like features
like the Peregrine soliton
[
59
]
,
or more complicated breathers constructed via algebraic-integrable techniques, see e.g.
[
9
,
11
]
, and which tend asymptotically to plane waves for both large positive and negative times.
Such structures have been observed in wave tank experiments
[
16
]
.

The second theory –dispersive focusing– predicts that rogue waves form through the superposition of weakly interacting waves whose phases nearly align at a given location, producing constructive interference, which has also been reproduced in laboratory wave tank experiments
[
14
,
22
]
.

A common approach to study dispersive focusing is
to model the water surface profile
\eta(t,x)
as a superposition of independent Gaussian waves and then compute the probability of forming a large-amplitude crest. Several theoretical and numerical studies (see e.g.
[
53
,
48
,
55
,
58
,
35
]
and references therein) predict that
if
the free surface profile
\eta(t,x)
follows a Gaussian distribution
\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{R}}(0,\sigma^{2})
, then

\mathbb{P}\left(2\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\eta(t,x)>{\mathtt{H}}_{0}\right)=\exp\left(-\frac{{\mathtt{H}}_{0}^{2}}{8\sigma^{2}}\right)\,.

(1.1)

Dematteis, Grafke, and Vanden-Eijnden
[
23
]
recently developed a novel probabilistic framework for the study of rogue waves, using large deviation principles, akin to (
1.1
), to identify the most likely extreme profiles.
The model was experimentally validated in
[
22
]
(and further verified in long wave tanks or through oceanic time series, as referenced in
[
42
,
50
,
38
]
).

The goal of this paper is to rigorously justify the statistic (
1.1
)
for the full
pure gravity water waves equations
in deep water –the most accurate quasilinear nonlocal free boundary problem modeling the surface motion–
when the initial datum is a random Gaussian
with small variance
\sigma\ll 1
, over very long times. We will achieve this through a large deviation principle for PDEs, thereby rigorously justifying the formal analysis of
[
23
]
in the weakly nonlinear setting.
Roughly, our main result
states the following (see
1.1
for a precise version):

\bullet

Let
\eta(t,x)
be the free water waves surface with a smooth random initial datum
\eta^{\omega}(0,x)

distributed as a Gaussian random variable with zero average and standard deviation
\sigma\ll 1
. Then,
for any
\sigma\ll{\mathtt{H}}_{0}\ll 1
and any time
|t|\leq{\mathtt{H}}_{0}^{-3+}
, the probability

{\mathbb{P}}\left(2\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\eta(t,x)>{\mathtt{H}}_{0}\right)\sim\exp\left(-\frac{{\mathtt{H}}_{0}^{2}}{8\sigma^{2}}\right)\ \hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ as }{\mathtt{H}}_{0}\to 0^{+}\,.

(1.2)

This statement will follow directly from

1.1
by setting
{\mathtt{H}}_{0}\approx\varepsilon^{1-\delta}
, where
0<\delta\ll 1

and by considering the variance of

\eta^{\omega}(0,x)
to be approximately
\varepsilon^{2}
, see the first comment below
1.1
.
Note that a wave
\eta(t,x)
with crest
{\mathtt{H}}_{0}/2
is
considered a rogue wave because, initially,
the expected value

\mathbb{E}(\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\eta^{\omega}(0,x))

has much smaller size
\sigma\ll{\mathtt{H}}_{0}
. The condition
{\mathtt{H}}_{0}\ll 1
corresponds to the weakly nonlinear regime of the water waves dynamics. The time interval
|t|\leq{\mathtt{H}}_{0}^{-3+}

is the optimal one for which the existence of water wave solutions is guaranteed by the deterministic well-posedness theory

[
5
,
26
,
69
]
, starting from
sufficiently small and smooth initial data of size

{\mathtt{H}}_{0}
in some
high Sobolev norm.
Deterministic energy estimates imply that
such Sobolev norm
remains of size
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathtt{H}}_{0})
over
the entire time interval of existence.
This does not contradict the statement (
1.2
)
that the

L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
norm of the water waves profile
increases with time.
The timescale
|t|\leq{\mathtt{H}}_{0}^{-3+}

is likely optimal because of the quintic wave
resonant interactions identified by
Craig-Worfolk
[
21
]
and Dyachenko-Lvov-Zakharov
[
30
]
,
that break the Birkhoff integrability
of the water waves equation,
and may lead to unstable dynamics.

The proof of (
1.2
) is highly nontrivial. The main difficulty is that, even if the initial free surface
\eta^{\omega}(0,x)
is Gaussian (together with the initial velocity potential
\psi^{\omega}(0,x)
in (
1.13
)), the surface profile
\eta(t,x)

does
not
necessarily remain Gaussian at later times. In fact, no such property is expected, since
\eta(t,x)
depends in a highly nonlinear way on the initial data. Tracking the evolution of a probability measure under a Hamiltonian PDE is a well-known challenge in PDEs and probability. In particular, invariant Gibbs measures have only been rigorously constructed for some semilinear PDEs (see Bourgain
[
12
]
and the recent breakthrough papers by Deng-Nahmod-Yue
[
28
,
29
]
, and the references therein), while the water waves equations are quasilinear. The theory of quasi-invariant measures offers an alternative approach based on studying the absolute continuity of the probability measure with respect to the initial Gaussian measure
[
67
,
56
]
.
These techniques can sometimes yield upper bounds on the tails of the probability measure
[
39
,
61
]
, but they are not developed for quasilinear systems and do not yet yield sharp upper/lower bounds such as (
1.2
).
Considerable progress has also been achieved in the context of wave turbulence theory, where the probability distribution of the solution to semilinear dispersive equations on large tori are tracked via its moments, which are proved to satisfy kinetic equations, see for instance
[
15
,
24
,
25
]
in the random data and
[
41
,
64
]
in the stochastic setting. These results are based on a Picard iteration scheme which is only convergent (without loss of derivatives) in the context of semilinear PDEs. Progress in the wave turbulence theory of the water waves system on large tori has been recently obtained by Deng-Ionescu-Pusateri in
[
26
,
27
]
– while they prove that, up to a set of exponentially small probability, the
L^{\infty}
-norm remains small over long timescales, our work explores the complementary event.
We further comment on this work in point
6
under
1.1
below.

In this article, we address the issue of the evolution of the tails of the probability measure under the water waves flow using different methods depending on the timescales.

•

For very long times
|t|\lesssim\varepsilon^{-5/2+}
, we construct a sufficiently accurate approximate solution of
the water waves flow which
propagates the Gaussianity
with time-dependent phase corrections, cf.

Section

4
.

This construction exploits the famous integrability of the pure gravity water waves Birkhoff normal form proved formally by
Zakharov-Dyachenko
[
72
]
, and rigorously in
Berti-Feola-Pusateri
[
5
]
.
This strategy is inspired by the approach Garrido, Grande, Kurianski, and Staffilani
[
37
]
developed
for the cubic 1d-NLS, with various key differences
commented in point
5
below Theorem
1.1
.

The main novelty lies in the
dynamical and
probabilistic
mechanism
developed to reach the timescale
|t|\lesssim\varepsilon^{-3+}
,
which is very different from the approach in
[
37
]
for NLS.

•

Up to optimal times

|t|\lesssim\varepsilon^{-3+}
, we do not propagate Gaussianity of the Fourier coefficients of some approximate solution, but we are still able to propagate the Gaussianity of the tails of the
L^{\infty}
-norm of the solution, and compute the probability that the nonlinear phases quasi-synchronize at a given time.

In particular, we do not attempt to track the probability distribution of the approximate solution’s nonlinear phases: this seems an
impossible task given the complex, nonlinear relation between the initial datum and the phases’ time-evolution.
Instead, we exploit this dependence to compute the probability that the initial phases will lead to quasi-synchronization (at any time), creating a constructive superposition of waves that produces a rogue wave, cf.
Section

5
.
We achieve this in two steps, as detailed in Section
1.2
.
Exact synchronization is
accomplished using a random version of the celebrated Brouwer fixed point theorem.
However, this represents a zero-probability event.
Instead quasi-synchronization, on a set of exponentially small probability, is achieved by proving quantitative Lipschitz estimates for the deterministic flow.
A final key step is to prove a weak version of statistical independence between the random amplitudes of the initial Fourier coefficients and the quasi-synchronization event (factorization property).
All in all, the formation of a rogue wave requires the quasi-synchronization of the phases of the first

{\mathtt{N}}_{\varepsilon}
Fourier modes. Here,

N_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\infty
, as slowly as desired, as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
.
Naturally this occurs with
an exponentially small probability.

In this result dispersive focusing
is identified as the dominant formation mechanism of rogue waves up to the timescale
|t|\lesssim{\mathtt{H}}_{0}^{-3+}
, establishing the lower bound
in (
1.1
).

1.2
complements this result by showing a large deviation principle for the quasi-synchronization of arbitrarily finitely many phases of the full water wave solution, not just the approximate one.

This method provides a new paradigm to quantifying the tail probability of solutions of
Hamiltonian PDEs with an integrable normal form and random Gaussian initial data, especially in the context of quasilinear PDEs for which invariant or quasi-invariant measures are not available (out-of-equilibrium).

1.1
Main results

To precisely state the main results, we first present the water waves equations in detail.
We consider the Euler equations of hydrodynamics for a 2-dimensional
incompressible and irrotational fluid under the action of

pure gravity
.
The fluid
occupies the time dependent region

\mathcal{D}_{\eta}:=\big\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}\ :\ y<\eta(t,x)\big\}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathbb{T}:=\mathbb{T}_{x}:=\mathbb{R}/(2\pi\mathbb{Z})\,,

(1.3)

with infinite depth and
space periodic boundary conditions.
The unknowns of the problem are the free surface
y=\eta(t,x)

of the domain
\mathcal{D}_{\eta}
and the
divergence free velocity field
\begin{pmatrix}u(t,x,y)\\
v(t,x,y)\end{pmatrix}
.
The velocity field is irrotational and therefore is
the gradient of a harmonic function
\Phi(t,x,y)
, called the generalized velocity potential.
We study the water waves equations
in the Hamiltonian
formulation developed by Zakharov
[
70
]

and Craig-Sulem
[
20
]
.
Denoting
\psi(t,x):=\Phi(t,x,\eta(t,x))

the evaluation of the generalized velocity potential at the free interface,
the velocity potential
\Phi

is recovered
by solving the elliptic problem

\Delta\Phi=0\ \mbox{ in }\mathcal{D}_{\eta}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\Phi=\psi\ \mbox{ at }y=\eta(t,x)\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\Phi_{y}\to 0\ \mbox{ as }y\to-\infty\,.

(1.4)

Inside
{\mathcal{D}}_{\eta}
the velocity field of the fluid evolves according to
the Euler equations and the
dynamics is
determined by
two boundary conditions at the free surface.
We impose
that the fluid particles at the time dependent
profile remain on it along the evolution
(kinematic boundary condition), and that
the pressure of the fluid
is equal to the constant
atmospheric pressure at the free surface (dynamic boundary condition). Thus the
time evolution of the fluid is determined by the following
system of quasi-linear equations

\begin{cases}{\partial}_{t}\eta=G(\eta)\psi\\
\displaystyle{{\partial}_{t}\psi=-g\eta-\frac{\psi_{x}^{2}}{2}+\frac{(\eta_{x}\psi_{x}+G(\eta)\psi)^{2}}{2(1+\eta_{x}^{2})}}\end{cases}

(1.5)

where
g>0
is the gravity constant which,
without loss of generality, we set equal to
g=1
, and

G(\eta)
is the Dirichlet-Neumann operator

G(\eta)\psi:=\sqrt{1+\eta_{x}^{2}}\,(\partial_{\vec{n}}\Phi)|_{y=\eta(t,x)}=(-\Phi_{x}\eta_{x}+\Phi_{y})|_{y=\eta(t,x)}\,,

(1.6)

here
\vec{n}
denotes the outer unit normal of
{\mathcal{D}}_{\eta}
.
The name refers to the fact that
G(\eta)

transforms the Dirichlet datum
\psi

of the velocity potential
\Phi
into its Neumann derivative
G(\eta)\psi
at the free surface.
Since
G(\eta)\psi
has zero
space average also

\langle\eta\rangle:=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta(x)\,{\rm d}x

is a first integral of (
1.5
)
and
we may consider
\eta(t,\cdot)
to evolve within a Sobolev space

H^{s}_{0}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}):=\left\{\eta(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\big(\alpha_{n}\cos(nx)+\beta_{n}\sin(nx)\big)\ \colon\hskip 10.00002pt\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{H^{s}_{0}}^{2}:=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}n^{2s}(\alpha_{n}^{2}+\beta_{n}^{2})<+\infty\right\}

(1.7)

of zero average functions.
In addition, the water waves vector field
X(\eta,\psi)=(X^{(\eta)},X^{(\psi)})

defined by the right hand of (
1.5
) depends only on

\eta
and the mean free component

\psi-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\psi\,{\rm d}x
. Thus
we may consider
\psi
to evolve within a
homogeneous Sobolev space
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})
obtained identifying
functions which differ by a constant.

As observed by Zakharov
[
70
]

the water waves equations (
1.5
) are the Hamiltonian system

{\partial}_{t}\eta=\nabla_{\psi}H(\eta,\psi)\,,\hskip 10.00002pt{\partial}_{t}\psi=-\nabla_{\eta}H(\eta,\psi)\,,

(1.8)

where
\nabla_{\eta},\nabla_{\psi}
denote the
L^{2}
-gradient, and the Hamiltonian

H(\eta,\psi)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big(\psi\,G(\eta)\psi+\eta^{2}\Big){\rm d}{x}

(1.9)

is the sum of the kinetic energy of the fluid
plus the potential energy due to gravity.

In addition to being Hamiltonian,
the water wave system (
1.5
)
is time-reversible with respect to the involution

\rho\begin{bmatrix}\eta(x)\\
\psi(x)\end{bmatrix}:=\begin{bmatrix}\eta(-x)\\
-\psi(-x)\end{bmatrix}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{i.e.}\ X\circ\rho=-\rho\circ X\,,

where
X=(X^{(\eta)},X^{(\psi)})=(\nabla_{\psi}H,-\nabla_{\eta}H)

is the water waves vector field.
Equivalently, the solution map
\Psi^{t}
of (
1.5
) (whenever defined)
satisfies
\Psi^{-t}=\rho\circ\Psi^{t}\circ\rho
(the flow
\Psi^{t}(\eta_{0},\psi_{0})
is defined as the unique solution of
(
1.5
) with initial condition

(\eta_{0},\psi_{0})
). In other words the solutions of (
1.5
) for
t<0
are obtained from solutions for positive times
by conjugation under
\rho
.
Furthermore,
the water waves system
(
1.5
)
is translation invariant, namely

X\circ\tau_{\varsigma}=\tau_{\varsigma}\circ X\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\varsigma\in\mathbb{R}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003pt\tau_{\varsigma}\colon f(x)\mapsto f(x+\varsigma)

(1.10)

is the translation operator.
This implies, by the Noether theorem,
the existence of the conserved quantity

\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta_{x}(x)\psi(x)\,{\rm d}x
, called momentum.

Let us also discuss regularity
properties of the water waves system (
1.5
), which we shall use.
Several results about the analyticity of the Dirichlet-Neumann operator
G(\eta)
acting
between Sobolev spaces of functions, with respect to the free
boundary
\eta(x)
, have been
proved in
[
19
,
52
]
,
to which we refer for an extended
bibliography. These results mainly concern the case of localized profiles and velocity potentials in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
In this paper we use the analyticity
result in
[
8
]
valid
under periodic boundary conditions in
infinite depth.
Let
B^{\mathtt{s}}(r)

denote the open ball in
H^{\mathtt{s}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})
of center
0
and radius
r>0
.
For
{\mathtt{s}}>\frac{7}{2}
,
{\mathtt{s}}+\frac{1}{2}\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
r>0
small enough, such that the Dirichlet-Neumann operator mapping

\eta\mapsto G(\eta)\,,\hskip 10.00002ptH^{\mathtt{s}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\cap B^{\frac{7}{2}}(r)\to{\cal L}(H^{\mathtt{s}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}),H^{{\mathtt{s}}-1}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}))\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{is analytic}\,.

(1.11)

Actually the result in
[
8
]

also holds
when
\eta
is analytic, and
G(\eta)
acts between spaces of analytic functions.
In view of (
1.11
) and the algebra properties of Sobolev spaces, for
{\mathtt{s}}>\frac{7}{2}
,
the water waves vector field
in (
1.5
)

(\eta,\psi)\mapsto X(\eta,\psi)=(X^{(\eta)},X^{(\psi)})\,,\hskip 10.00002ptB^{{\mathtt{s}}}(r)\times H^{{\mathtt{s}}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\to H^{{\mathtt{s}}-1}_{0}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\times{\dot{H}}^{{\mathtt{s}}-1}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{is analytic}\,,

(1.12)

possibly with a smaller
r>0
.

The local well-posedness theory for these equations is a challenging problem. The major difficulties stem from the quasilinear and nonlocal nature of the vector field in (
1.5
), and a “hidden” hyperbolic structure within the equations.
The first existence results
with initial data in Sobolev spaces
were established by S. Wu
in the pioneering work
[
68
]
.
In the Eulerian formulation (
1.5
), local well posedness was later achieved by Lannes

[
51
]
and Alazard-Burq-Zuily
[
1
]
,
through the introduction of the
“good unknown” of Alinhac

\upomega
, instead of
\psi
, and
a paralinearization of the Dirichlet-Neumann operator.
This change of variables
reveals the hyperbolic nature of the water
waves equations.
The paradifferential approach
[
1
]

enables to prove the existence of water waves solutions in

(\eta,\psi)\in C^{0}\left([0,t],H^{{\mathtt{s}}}_{0}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\times\dot{H}^{\mathtt{s}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\right)\,,\hskip 10.00002pt{{\mathtt{s}}}>\tfrac{3}{2}\,,

under weaker regularity assumptions on the Cauchy data than in previous works.
We shall present
the deterministic water waves
theory in
Part

I
.
These results,
when specialized to initial data of size
\varepsilon
,
imply a time of existence larger than
c\varepsilon^{-1}
.
Actually the water waves exist
up to times
\varepsilon^{-3}
,
provided the initial datum is sufficiently regular, as first proved by the paradifferential Birkhoff normal form in
[
5
]
, see also
[
69
,
26
]
.
These works ultimately rely on the celebrated integrability
of the pure gravity water waves equations in deep water up to quartic vector fields,
as formally guessed by
Zakharov and Dyachenko
[
72
]
.
The approach in
[
5
]

to rigorously justify the formal computations in
[
72
]

is based on a uniqueness argument of the normal form:
in view of non-resonance properties satisfied by the linear frequencies,
the “paradifferential” normal and the formal one
in
[
72
]

have to coincide up to cubic degree.
Other deterministic long time existence results
for periodic water waves are in

[
69
,
46
,
43
,
4
,
3
,
7
]

and references therein.

Random initial data.

In this paper we study the evolution of the water waves equations (
1.5
) starting from
small amplitude
random
initial data

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\eta_{0}^{\omega}(x)=\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{\pi}}\,\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}c_{n}\left(\alpha_{n}^{\omega}\cos(nx)+\beta_{n}^{\omega}\sin(nx)\right)\\
\psi_{0}^{\omega}(x)=\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{\pi}}\,\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}d_{n}\left(\gamma_{n}^{\omega}\cos(nx)+\delta_{n}^{\omega}\sin(nx)\right)\end{cases}

(1.13)

\displaystyle\mbox{ where }(\alpha^{\omega}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},(\beta_{n}^{\omega})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},(\gamma_{n}^{\omega})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},(\delta_{n}^{\omega})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mbox{ are i.i.d. real Gaussian r.v. }\sim{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbb{R}}\left(0,1\right)\,,

in the probability space
(\mathbb{\Omega},{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{P}})
, and
where the deterministic
coefficients
c_{n},d_{n}
are

c_{j}:=e^{-|j|{\mathtt{b}}}\,,\ j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{for some }{\mathtt{b}}>0\,,\hskip 10.00002ptd_{n}:=n^{-1/2}c_{n}=n^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-n{\mathtt{b}}}\ \ \ \,\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\,.

(1.14)

The specific choice of
c_{j}
in (
1.14
) is introduced for simplicity to
guarantee that
\eta_{0}^{\omega}
belongs to any
H_{0}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})
almost surely for any
s>0

with
s
sufficiently large so that the deterministic normal form theory is available.

For any
x\in\mathbb{T}
,
\eta_{0}^{\omega}(x)
is a Gaussian random variable
\sim\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{R}}(0,\varepsilon^{2}\pi^{-1}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}c_{n}^{2})
,
while
\psi_{0}^{\omega}(x)
is a Gaussian random variable

\sim\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{R}}(0,\varepsilon^{2}\pi^{-1}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}d_{n}^{2})
.
Indeed,
\mathbb{E}\eta_{0}^{\omega}(x)=\mathbb{E}\psi_{0}^{\omega}(x)=0
, which means that the average initial profile of the fluid is flat and the ocean is, on average, initially at rest.
Furthermore, the independence of the random Gaussian variables implies that
the variances of the initial wave
surface and velocity potential are

\mathbb{E}(\eta_{0}^{\omega}(x))^{2}=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\pi}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}c_{n}^{2}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\mathbb{E}(\psi_{0}^{\omega}(x))^{2}=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\pi}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}d_{n}^{2}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\forall x\in\mathbb{T}\,,

(1.15)

so the typical size of the
initial data is
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
. It is convenient to define

{\boldsymbol{\upsigma}}^{2}:=\frac{1}{\pi}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}c_{n}^{2}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}c_{j}^{2}\ ,\hskip 20.00003pt\mbox{ so that }\eta_{0}^{\omega}(x)\sim\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{R}}(0,\varepsilon^{2}{\boldsymbol{\upsigma}}^{2})\,.

(1.16)

Furthermore,
in view of the exponential decay of the
c_{n}
in (
1.14
) and the assumption

d_{n}=n^{-\frac{1}{2}}c_{n}
,
the initial data belong almost surely
to any Sobolev space and

\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\eta_{0}^{\omega}\right\rVert_{H_{0}^{s}}^{2}=\varepsilon^{2}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}c_{n}^{2}\,n^{2s}=\varepsilon^{2}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}d_{n}^{2}n^{2s+1}=\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\psi_{0}^{\omega}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for any}\ s\geq 0\,.

Linear water waves.

The linearized water waves system
(
1.5
) at the equilibrium
(\eta,\psi)=(0,0)
is

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\eta&=|D|\psi\\
\partial_{t}\psi&=-\eta\,,\end{cases}

(1.17)

where the Dirichlet-Neumann operator
G(0)

at the flat surface
\eta=0
is
the Fourier multiplier
G(0)=|D|
.
In the linear complex variable

\zeta:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}|D|^{-\frac{1}{4}}\eta+\frac{{\rm i}}{\sqrt{2}}|D|^{\frac{1}{4}}\psi\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\hskip 10.00002pt\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\psi\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{2}\,{\rm Re}\,|D|^{\frac{1}{4}}\zeta\\
\sqrt{2}\,{\rm Im}\,|D|^{-\frac{1}{4}}\zeta\end{pmatrix}\,,

(1.18)

the real system (
1.17
)
is transformed into the equation

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}\zeta=-{\rm i}\Omega(D)\zeta\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\Omega(D):=|D|^{\frac{1}{2}}\,,

(1.19)

which, written in Fourier, amounts to
infinitely many
decoupled harmonic oscillators

\dot{\zeta}_{j}=-{\rm i}\,\Omega_{j}\zeta_{j}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\Omega_{j}:=|j|^{\frac{1}{2}}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\forall j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}\,.

(1.20)

In view of (
1.18
), recalling the definitions of the Sobolev norms (
1.7
) and (
1.41
), we have

\|(\eta,\psi)\|_{H^{s}_{0}\times\dot{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}:=\|\eta\|_{H^{s}_{0}}^{2}+\|\psi\|_{\dot{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}=2\|\zeta\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{4}}}^{2}\,.

(1.21)

The solution of (
1.20
) with initial datum

\zeta_{0j}
is the traveling plane wave

\zeta_{0j}e^{-{\rm i}(\Omega_{j}t-jx)}
.
The assumption

c_{n}=n^{\frac{1}{2}}d_{n}
in (
1.14
)
implies that
the complex initial datum

\zeta_{0}^{\omega}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}|D|^{-\frac{1}{4}}\eta_{0}^{\omega}+\frac{{\rm i}}{\sqrt{2}}|D|^{\frac{1}{4}}\psi_{0}^{\omega}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\zeta_{0j}^{\omega}e^{{\rm i}jx}\,,

(1.22)

has Fourier coefficients

\zeta_{0j}^{\omega}
that
are
independent complex Gaussians
random variables

{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbb{C}}\big(0,\varepsilon^{2}\,c_{j}^{2}\,|j|^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big)
, for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
,
as we prove in
3.2
.
The solution of
the complex linear system (
1.19
) with initial datum
\zeta_{0}^{\omega}(x)
defined in (
1.22
) is

\zeta_{L}(t,x):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\zeta_{0j}^{\omega}\,e^{-{\rm i}\,\left(\Omega_{j}t-j\,x\right)}\,.

(1.23)

Inverting formula (
1.18
), we find that
the solution of the linear water waves system
(
1.17
) with
initial datum
(\eta_{0},\psi_{0})
is

\eta_{L}(t,x)=\sqrt{2}\,{\rm Re}\,|D|^{\frac{1}{4}}\zeta_{L}(t,x)\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\psi_{L}(t,x)=\sqrt{2}\,{\rm Im}\,|D|^{-\frac{1}{4}}\zeta_{L}(t,x)\,.

(1.24)

The
linear dynamics (
1.24
) preserves the Gaussianity of the initial data, unlike the nonlinear water waves flow.

Main results.

In this paper we are interested
in understanding the set of
random initial data
(\eta^{\omega}_{0}(x),\psi^{\omega}_{0}(x))

in (
1.13
) for which the deterministic water waves evolution
develops rogue waves at times
t>0
, namely the wave profile

\eta(t,x)

–which
has a typical initial amplitude of size
\varepsilon
–
will become larger than a threshold
\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}
,
\delta\in(0,1)
, at some time
t
.
The main
Theorem
1.1
below
estimates the probability of
the event (
1.25
) that the water waves solution
(\eta,\psi)
to (
1.5
) with initial data (
1.13
) exists in

C^{0}([0,t],H^{\mathtt{s}}_{0}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\times\dot{H}^{\mathtt{s}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}))
,
{\mathtt{s}}>\frac{3}{2}
,
up to a time
t
and develops a rogue wave

\mathfrak{R}^{t}(\lambda):=\Big\{\omega\in\mathbb{\Omega}\mid\exists\,\eta\in C^{0}([0,t],H^{\mathtt{s}}_{0})\ \mbox{and}\ \sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\eta(t,x)\geq\lambda\Big\}\,.

(1.25)

The physically more interesting case is when

\delta
is close to
1
, as in this case
the rogue wave crest profile is bigger.
Note also
that the sign of the wave profile
\eta(t,x)

in (
1.25
) is important because we are specifically interested in the
possible formation of high crests,
not deep troughs.
This is why we do not consider in (
1.25
) the absolute value of
\eta(t,x)
.

The first main result of this paper is the following large deviation principle for the formation of rogue waves.

Theorem 1.1

(
Rogue wave formation).

For any
\lambda_{0}>0
,
\delta\in(0,1)
and
0<\kappa\ll 1-\delta
, the free surface
\eta(t,x)
of the water waves solution of (
1.5
) with random initial datum (
1.13
) satisfies the large deviation principle

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}\varepsilon^{2\delta}\,\log{\mathbb{P}}\left(\mathfrak{R}^{t}(\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta})\right)=-\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}}{2{\boldsymbol{\upsigma}}^{2}}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\mathfrak{R}^{t}(\lambda)\mbox{ in }\eqref{eventr},\hskip 10.00002pt{\boldsymbol{\upsigma}}\mbox{ in }\eqref{upsigma}\,,

(1.26)

for any time
t
satisfying

(i)

|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-\frac{5}{2}(1-\delta)+\kappa}
;

(ii)

|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-3(1-\delta)+\kappa}
provided
\delta\in(\frac{3}{5},1)
.

1.1
is a corollary of
3.1
stated below.
Let us make some comments.

1.

Proof of the large deviation principle (
1.2
).

1.1
guarantees
the existence of initial data,
with exponentially small probability, which
lead to a rogue wave at time
t
. Specifically, the initial data have average size
{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)
,
as shown in (
1.15
), while the resulting
rogue wave crest at time
t
has significantly
larger size
{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{1-\delta})
.
The statement (
1.2
) follows immediately by setting
{\mathtt{H}}_{0}=2\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}
and
noting that the variance of
\eta_{0}^{\omega}(x)
is
{\varepsilon^{2}}\,{\boldsymbol{\upsigma}}^{2}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\varepsilon^{2}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}c_{j}^{2}
, see (
1.16
).

2.

Dispersive focusing.
As part of the proof of
1.1
, we construct in
Section

5
an explicit family of rogue-wave approximate solutions
whose phases synchronize
at time
t
, creating
a constructive superposition, see
5.5
.
Synchronization occurs across

{\mathtt{N}}:={\mathtt{N}}(\varepsilon)
phases, a number that is finite but grows polynomially with
\varepsilon^{-1}
,
and involves the lowest
2{\mathtt{N}}
Fourier modes of the truncated approximate solution. The contribution of the remaining high Fourier modes towards the rogue wave can be shown to be negligible, see
5.5
for details.

3.

No growth of Sobolev norms.
The rogue waves constructed in
this work do not feature any growth of Sobolev
H^{s}
-norms. Instead, their
H^{s}
-norms remain almost
constant over long timescales and are of size
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{1-\delta})
, as a consequence
of the energy estimates in
2.5
.
This does not contradict the dispersive focusing mechanism for the
L^{\infty}
-norm discussed in the previous item.

4.

Timescale
|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-3(1-\delta)+\kappa}
with
\kappa\ll 1-\delta
.

This timescale restriction
guarantees the deterministic well posedness of the water waves.
Since rogue waves in
\mathfrak{R}^{t}(\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta})
have size
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{1-\delta})
in a Sobolev space
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
, the deterministic theory
[
5
]
ensures the
existence of water waves solutions up to times

|t|\lesssim\varepsilon^{-3(1-\delta)}
(which we shrink to the slightly smaller
|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-3(1-\delta)+\kappa}
).
This timescale is likely optimal due to the quintic wave resonant interactions identified in

[
21
,
33
]
.
Such resonances could increase the likelihood of rogue wave formation over time, a phenomenon
recently demonstrated for
the 1d semilinear beating NLS in
[
40
]
. The timescale
\varepsilon^{-3(1-\delta)+}
in
(ii)
is clearly much longer than
\varepsilon^{-\frac{5}{2}(1-\delta)+}
in
(i)
for any fixed
\delta\in(0,1)
. The physically more interesting case is when

\delta
is close to
1
, as rogue waves are larger.

5.

Comparison with
[
37
]
.
We improve the results in
[
37
]
in several ways. First, Theorem
1.1
concerns the full water waves equations in deep water,
extending much beyond the semilinear NLS envelope approximation.
The quasilinear nature of the water waves vector field requires a paradifferential normal form to construct approximate solutions.
Another key difference is the
non-diagonal nature of the Zakharov-Dyachenko normal form
(
2.16
), unlike the NLS normal form. This significantly complicates the probabilistic argument to prove the Gaussianity of the first approximate solution (see Lemma
4.3
).
Furthermore, while the NLS result in

[
37
]
is valid only up to times
|t|\lesssim\varepsilon^{-2(1-\delta)}
,
a more refined energy estimate (Lemma
4.1
) is required to extend this result to longer times
|t|\lesssim\varepsilon^{-\frac{5}{2}(1-\delta)+\kappa}
.
In any case, the main novelty in this work lies in the new approach detailed in Section
5
, which employs a non-Gaussian and non-explicit approximation of the water wave dynamics. This framework, based on a random fixed point theorem and phase quasi-synchronization, is detailed in Section

1.2
below.

6.

Comparison with
[
26
,
27
]
.
Deng-Ionescu-Pusateri prove existence of solutions of the water waves
equations (
1.5
) with random initial data, in the weakly nonlinear regime, for
large tori
,
well beyond the deterministic results. A key part of their work is to propagate
{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{1-})
bounds for the
L^{\infty}
-norm of the solution with much larger
H^{s}
-norm, which they prove to be possible excluding a small set of size
{\mathcal{O}}(e^{-\varepsilon^{-a}})
,
0<a\ll 1
, of initial data.

1.1
can therefore be seen as a complementary result to
[
27
]
in the
unit torus
, as it proves the existence of an exponentially small set of random initial data for which the solution does develop a large
L^{\infty}
-norm.

1.1
is complemented by proving
that the asymptotic probability of rogue wave formation is identical to the asymptotic probability of the set of rogue waves that form due to the
quasi-synchronization
of the first
{\mathtt{M}}

phases
(\theta_{j}(t))_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{M}}}
of the free water profile

\eta(t,x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\,\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\rho_{j}(t)\,\cos(\theta_{j}(t)+jx)\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\rho_{j}(t)\geq 0\ .

(1.27)

To formalize such statement, consider the event that the water waves solution
(\eta,\psi)
to (
1.5
)
with random initial data (
1.13
) exists in

C^{0}([0,t],H^{\mathtt{s}}_{0}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\times\dot{H}^{\mathtt{s}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}))
,
{\mathtt{s}}>\frac{3}{2}
,
up to a time
t
and has the first
{\mathtt{M}}
phases quasi-syncronized, namely

\mathfrak{P}^{t}({\mathtt{M}},\epsilon):=\Big\{\omega\in\mathbb{\Omega}\mid\exists\,\eta\in C^{0}([0,t],H^{\mathtt{s}}_{0})\ \mbox{and}\ |\theta_{j}(t)\ \mathrm{mod}\ 2\pi|\leq\epsilon\hskip 10.00002pt\forall 0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{M}}\Big\}\,.

(1.28)

We prove the following result directly valid on the optimal time interval.

Theorem 1.2

(
Dispersive focusing).

For any
{\mathtt{M}}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\lambda_{0}>0
,
\delta\in(\frac{3}{5},1)
and
0<\kappa\ll 1-\delta
,
the free surface
\eta(t,x)
of the water waves solution of (
1.5
) with random initial datum (
1.13
)
satisfies
the following large deviation principle for any time
|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-3(1-\delta)+\kappa}
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}\varepsilon^{2\delta}\,\log{\mathbb{P}}\left(\mathfrak{R}^{t}(\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta})\cap\mathfrak{P}^{t}({\mathtt{M}},\varepsilon^{\frac{\kappa}{5}})\right)=-\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}}{2{\boldsymbol{\upsigma}}^{2}}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\mathfrak{R}^{t}(\lambda)\mbox{ in }\eqref{eventr},\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{P}^{t}({\mathtt{M}},\epsilon)\mbox{ in }\eqref{eventp}\,,

(1.29)

and
{\boldsymbol{\upsigma}}
in (
1.16
).

This result suggests that dispersive focusing is the dominant mechanism in rogue wave formation.

1.2
Ideas of the proof

Let us explain the main ideas of the proof of
3.1
, which implies
1.1
and the related
1.2
. As mentioned before,
it relies on deterministic long time existence results based on a paradifferential Birkhoff normal form jointly with novel probabilistic arguments to justify the dispersive focusing mechanism of formation of rogue
waves.

Well-posedness of solutions and normal form.

We begin by introducing the deterministic water wave theory.
For any
\delta,\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
{\mathtt{R}}>0
,
s\geq 0
, we define the ball of initial data

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{0}:={\mathcal{B}}_{0}(\varepsilon,\delta,{\mathtt{R}},s):=

\displaystyle\left\{(\eta_{0},\psi_{0})\in H^{s}_{0}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\times\dot{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\colon\hskip 10.00002pt\left\lVert(\eta_{0},\psi_{0})\right\rVert_{H_{0}^{s}\times\dot{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}\leq{\mathtt{R}}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\right\}\,,

(1.30)

\displaystyle\text{where}\hskip 10.00002pt\left\lVert(\eta_{0},\psi_{0})\right\rVert_{H_{0}^{s}\times\dot{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}:=\left\lVert\eta_{0}\right\rVert_{H_{0}^{s}}^{2}+\left\lVert\psi_{0}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}\,.

Following the common probabilistic perspective, when considering random initial data
(\eta_{0}^{\omega},\psi_{0}^{\omega})
as in (
1.13
), we shall still denote by
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
the event

{\mathcal{B}}_{0}:={\mathcal{B}}_{0}(\varepsilon,\delta,{\mathtt{R}},s):=\left\{\omega\in\mathbb{\Omega}\ :\ \left\lVert(\eta_{0}^{\omega},\psi_{0}^{\omega})\right\rVert_{H_{0}^{s}\times\dot{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}\leq{\mathtt{R}}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\right\}\,.

(1.31)

The fact that
\psi_{0}(x)
is more regular that

\eta_{0}(x)

in (
1.30
)-(
1.31
)
is due to the choice
d_{n}=c_{n}n^{-1/2}
in (
1.14
),
which implies the Gaussianity of the Fourier coefficients
\zeta_{0j}^{\omega}
in (
1.22
), as we explain in
3.2
.

The deterministic
theory of
[
1
,
5
]
guarantees that
any random initial datum in
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
, with
s>s_{0}\gg 1
sufficiently large, yields a solution to the water waves equations (
1.5
) that exists up to times
|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-3(1-\delta)+\kappa}
and remains small, as stated in (
2.37
) of

2.8
. The latter is established using the Birkhoff normal form which reduces the water wave equations to system (
2.22
),
as
proved in
[
5
]
. The main novelty of Theorem
2.4
,
compared to the original result in
[
5
]
,
is that the normal form map is a
phase space transformation
that satisfies the Lipschitz bounds (
2.14
) and (
2.20
). To establish these properties
we modify the construction of
[
5
]
, as
detailed in
Appendix

A
.
As a consequence, the map

(\eta_{0}^{\omega},\psi_{0}^{\omega})\mapsto z(t,x;\eta_{0}^{\omega},\psi_{0}^{\omega})\,,

(1.32)

which sends the initial data to the flow of the normal form equation (
2.22
) at time
t
, is Lipschitz continuous (see
2.8
-(
iv
)).
This
property is crucial for the probabilistic analysis developed in
Part

II
.

It is also important to note that
the complementary event
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{c}=\mathbb{\Omega}\setminus{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
, where
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}(\varepsilon,\delta,{\mathtt{R}},s)
is defined in
(
1.31
),
has negligible probability compared to that in (
1.26
)
(cf. Lemma
3.3
)

\log{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{c})\leq-\frac{{\mathtt{R}}^{2}}{4\|\vec{c}\|_{h^{s}}^{2}}\varepsilon^{-2\delta}+1\ ,\hskip 20.00003pt\mbox{ where }\ \|\vec{c}\|_{h^{s}}\ \mbox{ is defined in }\ \eqref{norm.c}\,.

(1.33)

As previously noted,
the primary challenge in proving
1.1
, is that the surface profile
\eta(t,x)
fails to preserve the Gaussianity of the initial data during time evolution.
We address this fundamental difficulty by employing different approximate solutions depending on the timescales, together with different methods to establish dispersive focusing.

Approximate solutions.

We construct approximate solutions
exploiting the cubic integrability of the deep water waves normal form system (
2.26
).
The first approximation that we use, see (
4.3
), is

\eta_{\rm app}(t,\cdot):=\sqrt{2}{\rm Re}\,|D|^{\frac{1}{4}}\,u_{\rm app}(t,\cdot)\ ,\hskip 10.00002ptu_{\rm app}(t,x):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}e^{-{\rm i}t{\mathcal{L}}_{j}(I(\zeta_{0}^{\omega}))+{\rm i}\phi_{j}^{\omega}}\,|\zeta_{0j}^{\omega}|\,e^{{\rm i}jx}\,,

(1.34)

where
\zeta_{0}^{\omega}(x)
is the initial datum in (
1.22
),

\phi_{j}^{\omega}
the phase of the Fourier coefficient
\zeta_{0j}^{\omega}

and
{\mathcal{L}}_{j}(I(\zeta_{0}^{\omega}))
the nonlinear normal form frequencies in (
2.24
).

4.1
demonstrates that
\eta_{\rm app}(t,x)

stays close in the
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
-norm to the water waves profile

\eta(t,x)
for times up to

\varepsilon^{-\frac{5}{2}(1-\delta)+\kappa}
. Furthermore

4.4
shows that
\eta_{\rm app}(t,x)
preserves the Gaussianity of the initial datum.
While the moduli of the Fourier coefficients of

u_{\rm app}(t,x)
are clearly Rayleigh-distributed (due to the Gaussianity of
\zeta_{0j}^{\omega}
), it is not immediately clear whether the nonlinear phases
-{\rm i}t{\mathcal{L}}_{j}(I(\zeta_{0}^{\omega}))+{\rm i}\phi_{j}^{\omega}
are uniformly distributed on the circle and independent from the moduli.
This difficulty arises because the nonlinear frequencies

{\mathcal{L}}_{j}(I(\zeta_{0}^{\omega}))
in (
2.24
)
are coupled across all infinitely many actions.

However
4.3
resolves this issue by
using conditional expectation arguments
which allow us to deduce the Gaussianity of the Fourier coefficients of
u_{\rm app}(t,x)
and,
consequently, the Gaussianity of
\eta_{\rm app}(t,x)
.
Then
3.1

(i)
on the shorter time interval
|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-\frac{5}{2}(1-\delta)+\kappa}
, follows from a large deviation principle proved in
[
37
]
for Gaussian random variables.

Proving
3.1

(ii)

up to the optimal timescales

|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-3(1-\delta)+\kappa}

requires a completely different approach, as the profile (
1.34
)
does not accurately approximate the free water wave surface

\eta(t,x)
over this interval.
The proof of
3.1

(ii)
relies on the improved approximation given by
(
5.1
), namely

\eta_{\rm app2}(t,\cdot):=\sqrt{2}{\rm Re}\,|D|^{\frac{1}{4}}\,u_{\rm app2}(t,\cdot)\,,\hskip 10.00002ptu_{\rm app2}(t,x):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\!\!\!\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\!\!\!e^{-{\rm i}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathcal{L}}_{j}(I(z(\tau;\eta_{0}^{\omega},\psi_{0}^{\omega}))){\rm d}\tau+{\rm i}\phi_{j}^{\omega}}\,|\zeta_{0j}^{\omega}|\,e^{{\rm i}jx}\,,

(1.35)

where the phases are now evaluated along the full trajectory of the solution
z(t;\eta_{0}^{\omega},\psi_{0}^{\omega})
of the normal form equation (
2.15
).

5.1
proves that
this new approximation stays close in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
-norm to the full water waves solution
\eta(t,x)
up to optimal times
|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-3(1-\delta)+\kappa}
, but
a drawback is the loss of control over its probability distribution.
Hence we develop a new method that allows to identify its tail distribution.

Phase synchronization.
According to the dispersive focusing mechanism, a rogue wave occurs as a result of constructive interference, i.e. the phases of its Fourier coefficients align at a given spatial point,
where the wave peaks increase its amplitude.
We make this mechanism
rigorous by computing the probability that,
at a fixed time
|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-3(1-\delta)+\kappa}
, the approximate solution (
1.35
) has
its first
{\mathtt{N}}\sim\varepsilon^{-\gamma}
,
\gamma>0
,
nonlinear phases nearly synchronized, thereby creating a peak at
x=0
(clearly this peak could form at any
point
x\in\mathbb{T}
).
Since the approximate solution is close to the true solution, this phase synchronization happens also for the full solution
\eta(t,x)
, which is the content of our
1.2
.

To synchronize the phases of the approximate solution (
1.35
), we
shall use the random Brouwer fixed point theorem by Bharucha-Reid and Mukherjea, see
[
10
, Theorem 10]
,
which we state as follows.

Theorem 1.3

(
Random Brouwer fixed point).

Let
(\mathbb{\Omega},{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{P}})
be a probability space and
K
be a compact, convex subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Consider a map

{\mathcal{T}}\colon\mathbb{\Omega}\times K\to K

satisfying

(i)

for any fixed
\omega\in\mathbb{\Omega}
, the map
{\mathcal{T}}(\omega,\cdot)\colon K\to K
is continuous;

(ii)

for any
\varphi\in K
, the map

{\mathcal{T}}(\cdot,\varphi)\colon\mathbb{\Omega}\to K
is measurable.

Then there exists a random variable
\varphi:(\mathbb{\Omega},{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{P}})\rightarrow K
such that

{\mathcal{T}}(\omega,\varphi^{\omega})=\varphi^{\omega}
a.s. in
\mathbb{\Omega}
.

Let us explain how we use this result in Section
5.2
.
The nonlinear phases
of the approximate solution (
1.35
),

\theta_{j}(t;\omega):=\phi_{j}^{\omega}-\int_{0}^{t}{\mathcal{L}}_{j}(I(z(\tau;\eta_{0}^{\omega},\psi_{0}^{\omega}))){\rm d}\tau\,,\hskip 20.00003ptj\in\mathbb{Z}\,,

depend in a highly nonlinear way on the initial random phases
(\phi_{j}^{\omega})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}
of the random initial data (
1.13
).
We seek the probability that the first
2{\mathtt{N}}
uniformly distributed random phases
(\phi_{j}^{\omega})_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}}
are chosen so that all
the
\theta_{j}(t;\omega)
,
0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}
,
align at zero at time
t
.

First step: alignment of first
2{\mathtt{N}}
phases for
partially randomized initial data.

We ask whether

at least one choice

of phases exists
that guarantees such alignment.
For this analysis, it is natural to treat the nonlinear phases
(\theta_{j}(t;\omega))_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}}

as depending
deterministically on
2{\mathtt{N}}
auxiliary initial phases
\vec{\phi}=(\phi_{j})_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}}\in[0,2\pi)^{2{\mathtt{N}}}
, and randomly on the initial moduli and remaining phases.
Precisely we introduce

\theta_{j}(t;\omega,\vec{\phi}):=\phi_{j}-{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(\omega,\vec{\phi})\ ,\hskip 10.00002pt{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(\omega,\vec{\phi}):=\int_{0}^{t}{\mathcal{L}}_{j}(I(z(\tau;{\widetilde{\eta}}_{0}^{\omega}(\cdot;\vec{\phi}),{\widetilde{\psi}}_{0}^{\omega}(\cdot;\vec{\phi})){\rm d}\tau\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{(mod}\ 2\pi)\,,\ 0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}\,,

(1.36)

where
({\widetilde{\eta}}_{0}^{\omega},{\widetilde{\psi}}_{0}^{\omega})(x;\vec{\phi})
is the partially randomized initial datum (
5.13
), whose
first
2{\mathtt{N}}
Fourier modes have deterministic phases
\vec{\phi}
(not the randomized ones
(\phi_{j}^{\omega})_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}}
), whereas all the Fourier moduli and the remaining
Fourier phases on the high modes
are randomized as in
(
1.13
).
Note that if the deterministic phases
(\phi_{j})_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}}
were randomized as independent uniform distributions, one would obtain the original randomization (
1.13
), i.e.
({\widetilde{\eta}}_{0}^{\omega},{\widetilde{\psi}}_{0}^{\omega})(x;\vec{\phi^{\omega}})=(\eta_{0}^{\omega},\psi_{0}^{\omega})
as stated in (
5.14
).

Having the phases

(\theta_{j}(t;\omega,\vec{\phi}))_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}}

in (
1.36
) aligned at zero at a fixed time
t
amounts to proving the existence of a fixed point of the random map

{\mathcal{T}}:=({\mathcal{T}}_{j})_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}}\colon{\mathcal{B}}_{0}\times K\to K\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for some}\ K\subset\mathbb{R}^{2{\mathtt{N}}}\ \text{compact}\,.

The crucial
5.3
proves that the map
{\mathcal{T}}
satisfies the assumptions of the random Brouwer fixed point theorem
1.3
.
Specifically the Lipschitzianity of the map
(
1.32
), demonstrated by the deterministic
2.8
, ensures
the continuity and measurability of
{\mathcal{T}}
.
Thus
1.3
guarantees
that

\text{there exists a r.v.}\ \vec{\phi}^{\ast,\omega}\in[0,2\pi)^{2{\mathtt{N}}}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{such that}\hskip 10.00002pt\theta_{j}(t;\omega,\vec{\phi}^{\ast,\omega})=0\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall 0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}\,,\ \text{a.e. in }\mathbb{\Omega}\,.

(1.37)

It is not trivial at all that the fixed point
\vec{\phi}^{\ast,\omega}
is a random variable,
as
Brouwer fixed points typically do not
exhibit continuous dependence on external parameters (i.e. the remaining Fourier phases and moduli).
Moreover, we note that

\vec{\phi}^{\ast,\omega}
is
independent
of the original
2{\mathtt{N}}
random uniformly distributed
initial phases
(\phi_{j}^{\omega})_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}}
by construction, a crucial property that we shall exploit in a moment, see
5.4
.

Second step: stability and factorization properties.

We now consider the set of events where
the uniformly distributed phases

\vec{\phi}^{\omega}:=(\vec{\phi}^{\omega}_{j})_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}}
are close to the random fixed point
\vec{\phi}^{\ast,\omega}
, namely the set

\mathcal{N}(\alpha):=\Big\{\omega\in\mathbb{\Omega}\mid\big|\phi^{\ast,\omega}_{j}-\phi^{\omega}_{j}\big|<\alpha\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall 0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}\Big\}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\alpha\in(0,\pi)\,.

(1.38)

We prove in
5.3
three crucial properties:

•

(a)
the set
\mathcal{N}(\alpha)
is measurable with probability
\left(\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\right)^{2{\mathtt{N}}}
.

•

(b)

stability property (
5.22
)
: for
any
\omega\in{\mathcal{N}}(\alpha)
the random phases
\vec{\phi}^{\omega}

generate nonlinear phases

(\theta_{j}(t;\omega,\vec{\phi}^{\omega}))_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}}
quasi-synchronized near zero, provided
\alpha
is sufficiently small, cf.
(
5.24
). Specifically
\alpha
is small with the inverse of the Lipschitz constant of the flow map (
1.32
), estimated in (
2.40
).

•

(c)

factorization property (
5.23
):

the probability of the intersection of
\mathcal{N}(\alpha)
with an event
{\mathcal{A}}

in the sub-
\sigma
-algebra generated by the moduli and the remaining phases, is the product of their probabilities.
The
factorization property (
5.23
)
ultimately follows from the independence of

\vec{\phi}^{\ast,\omega}
and
\vec{\phi}^{\omega}
.

Third step:
phase synchronization for the approximate solution.
In view of
(
1.37
) and the above stability property
(b)
,

5.5
proves that,
at any time
t
, within
\mathcal{N}(\alpha)
and provided
\alpha
is sufficiently small, the approximate solution
(
1.35
)
admits
the lower bound

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\eta_{\rm app2}^{\omega}(t,x)\geq{\frac{\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)}{\sqrt{\pi}}}\,\,\sum_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}}c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}+o(\varepsilon^{2-\delta})\,.

(1.39)

5.7

combines these ingredients to establish the following lower bound on the tail-statistics
of the crest of
\eta(t,x)
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\eta(t,x)\geq\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\Big)

\displaystyle\geq{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\eta_{\rm app2}^{\omega}(t,x)\geq\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}(1+o_{\varepsilon}(1))\Big)

\displaystyle\geq{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\big\{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\eta_{\rm app2}^{\omega}(t,x)\geq\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}(1+o_{\varepsilon}(1))\big\}\cap{\mathcal{N}}(\alpha)\Big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{2909:1522}}}{{\geq}}{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\Big\{\sum_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}}c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}\geq\sqrt{\pi}\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}(1+o_{\varepsilon}(1))\Big\}\cap{\mathcal{N}}(\alpha)\Big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\sum_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}}c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}\geq\sqrt{\pi}\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}(1+o_{\varepsilon}(1))\Big)\,{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{N}}(\alpha))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\sum_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}}c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}\geq\sqrt{\pi}\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}(1+o_{\varepsilon}(1))\Big)\,\left(\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\right)^{2{\mathtt{N}}}\,.

The tail-statistics of the last set is readily computed by
4.5
.
The choice of
\alpha
in
(
5.45
) guarantees that
\left(\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\right)^{2{\mathtt{N}}}=\exp({\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{-3(1-\delta)-\gamma}))
,
which is larger than

\exp(-\lambda_{0}^{2}\varepsilon^{-2\delta}/2{\boldsymbol{\upsigma}}^{2})
provided
0<\gamma<5\delta-3
. This finally proves, for
any
\delta>\frac{3}{5}
,

3.1

(ii)
.

Organization of the paper.

In Part
I

we report the deterministic
well posedness theory of water waves equations for small
periodic initial data. Because
of the quasilinearity of
the equations this requires delicate energy estimates
based on paradifferential calculus and
the normal form Theorem
2.4

that we prove in Appendix

A
.
In Part
II
we prove the large deviation Theorem
3.1
, which implies

1.1
.
The primary innovation is the new rogue wave formation mechanism, detailed in the extensive
Section

5
, which relies on an unconventional combination of a deterministic normal form approximation and a random fixed-point argument to show phase quasi-synchronization.
We include Section
4
because its easier argument offers a valuable opportunity to integrate expertise from both PDEs and Probability.
In
Section

5.4
we prove Theorem
1.2
.

Notation.

We denote
\mathbb{N}=\{1,2,\ldots\}
the natural numbers and

\mathbb{N}_{0}:=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
.
We write
a\lesssim b
to indicate an estimate of the form
a\leq cb
for some constant
c>0
which may change from line to line. For functions
f,g:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
of some variable
\varepsilon>0
, we write
f(\varepsilon)\ll g(\varepsilon)
or
f(\varepsilon)=o(g(\varepsilon))
to
mean
\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}f(\varepsilon)/g(\varepsilon)=0
. We also write
f(\varepsilon)=\mathcal{O}(g(\varepsilon))
meaning
f(\varepsilon)\lesssim g(\varepsilon)
for an implicit constant which is independent on
\varepsilon
.
We denote by
h^{s}
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
, the Hilbert space
of sequences
\vec{c}:=(c_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}
,
c_{j}\in\mathbb{C}
, with norm

\|\vec{c}\|_{h^{s}}^{2}:={\mathop{\sum}}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}|j|^{2s}\,|c_{j}|^{2}\,.

(1.40)

We set
\|\vec{c}\|_{\ell^{\infty}}:=\sup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\,|c_{j}|
.
We expand a periodic function
u:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
in Fourier series as

u(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}u_{j}e^{{\rm i}jx}\,,\hskip 10.00002ptu_{j}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}u(x)\,e^{-{\rm i}jx}\,{\rm d}x\,,

and we often identify

u(x)
with the sequence of
its Fourier coefficients

(u_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
.
We denote by
H^{s}:=H^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C})
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
, the Hilbert space of
u
such that
(u_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\in h^{s}
.
The subspace
H^{s}_{0}\subset H^{s}
, resp.

{\dot{H}}^{s}:=\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C})
, consists of those functions in
H^{s}
with zero average, resp. equivalent classes modulo constants, with norm

\|u\|_{H_{0}^{s}}=\|u\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}=\Big({\mathop{\sum}}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}|u_{j}|^{2}|j|^{2s}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,.

(1.41)

Finally, we define the Banach spaces
\mathcal{F}L^{s,p}
,
s\geq 0
,
p\in[1,\infty)
, of
periodic functions

u:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
with norm

\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}L^{s,p}}=\Big({\mathop{\sum}}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}|u_{j}|^{p}|j|^{sp}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\,.

(1.42)

Random variables.

We consider a probability space

(\mathbb{\Omega},{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{P}})
where
\mathbb{\Omega}
is a measure space
with a
\sigma
-algebra
{\mathcal{F}}
and
{\mathbb{P}}
is a probability measure, i.e.
{\mathbb{P}}(\mathbb{\Omega})=1
.
We consider random variables (r.v.), namely measurable functions

X:(\mathbb{\Omega},{\mathcal{F}})\to(\mathbb{R},{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbb{R}))\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\omega\mapsto X^{\omega}\,,

where
{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbb{R})

is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathbb{R}
. The law or distribution of
X
is the push-forward measure of
{\mathbb{P}}
under
X
and it is characterized by the
cumulative distribution function

F_{X}(x):={\mathbb{P}}(X\leq x)
.
We denote by
\sigma(X):=\{X^{-1}(B)\colon B\in{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbb{R})\}
the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
X
,
and by
\sigma(X,Y)
the smallest
\sigma
-algebra containing
\sigma(X)\cup\sigma(Y)
.
Two
\sigma
-algebras
{\mathcal{G}}_{1},{\mathcal{G}}_{2}
are independent iff
{\mathbb{P}}(A_{1}\cap A_{2})={\mathbb{P}}(A_{1}){\mathbb{P}}(A_{2})
for any
A_{j}\in{\mathcal{G}}_{j}
,
j=1,2
.
Two random variables
X,Y

are
independent
iff
\sigma(X)
and
\sigma(Y)
are independent. We write
X\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y
.
It may happen that the distribution of

X^{\omega}
is assigned via a

probability density function

f(x)
(p.d.f.),
namely a non-negative function
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

{\mathbb{P}}(X\leq x)=\int_{-\infty}^{x}f(y)\,{\rm d}y
.
In this case

{\mathbb{P}}(X\in[a,b])=\int_{a}^{b}f(x)\,{\rm d}x
.
The
expected value
of the random variable
X
is

\mathbb{E}X:=\int_{\mathbb{\Omega}}X^{\omega}{\rm d}{\mathbb{P}}(\omega)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}xf(x)\,{\rm d}x

and its
variance

is
\text{Var}(X):=\mathbb{E}(X-\mathbb{E}X)^{2}
.
If
X,Y
are independent then
\mathbb{E}(XY)=\mathbb{E}(X)\mathbb{E}(Y)
.

Given a sub-
\sigma
-algebra
{\mathcal{G}}\subset{\mathcal{F}}
and a random variable
X^{\omega}
in
L^{1}(\mathbb{\Omega},{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{P}})
, we denote by
\mathbb{E}[X^{\omega}\mid{\mathcal{G}}]
the conditional expectation of
X^{\omega}
with respect to
{\mathcal{G}}
. When
{\mathcal{G}}
is generated by another r.v.
Y^{\omega}
, i.e.
{\mathcal{G}}=\sigma(Y^{\omega})
, we will simply write
\mathbb{E}[X^{\omega}|Y^{\omega}]
to denote
\mathbb{E}[X^{\omega}\mid{\mathcal{G}}]
. See
[
62
, Sections 10.1-10.2]
for the definition and main properties.

Remark 1.4
.

We record here the two fundamental properties of conditional expectation that we will use in this work. Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a
\sigma
-algebra,
{\mathcal{G}}
a sub
\sigma
-algebra of
{\mathcal{F}}
, and
X\in L^{1}(\mathbb{\Omega},\mathcal{F},{\mathbb{P}})
. Then

(i)

Tower property:

\mathbb{E}[X]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[X|{\mathcal{G}}]]
.

(ii)

“Take out what is known”:
If
Y\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{\Omega},\mathcal{F},{\mathbb{P}})
is
{\mathcal{G}}
-measurable, then
\mathbb{E}[YX|{\mathcal{G}}]=Y\mathbb{E}[X|{\mathcal{G}}]
.

As is customary in probability theory, when considering random initial data
we use the notation
(\eta_{0}^{\omega},\psi_{0}^{\omega})\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
where
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
is the ball in (
1.30
), to mean
\omega\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
where
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
is the corresponding event in (
1.31
).

We also recall the
following elementary definitions.

Definition 1.5
.

Let
\sigma\neq 0
. A random variable
X^{\omega}
is a

1)

Real Gaussian r.v.
\sim{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbb{R}}(0,\sigma^{2})
if its p.d.f is
f_{\sigma}(x)=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}
. Its expected value is
0
and its variance
is
\sigma^{2}
.

2)

Complex Gaussian r.v.
\sim{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbb{C}}(0,\sigma^{2})
if

X^{\omega}=\dfrac{a^{\omega}+{\rm i}b^{\omega}}{\sqrt{2}}
with
a^{\omega}
,
b^{\omega}
independent real Gaussian variables in
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbb{R}}(0,\sigma^{2})
(note that

a^{\omega}
and
b^{\omega}
have the same variance).

3)

Rayleigh r.v.
\sim\mathscr{R}(\sigma)
if
its p.d.f is

f_{\sigma}(x)=\dfrac{x}{\sigma^{2}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}
for any
x\geq 0
and
f_{\sigma}(x)=0
for any
x<0
.

4)

Uniform r.v.
\sim\mathscr{U}([0,2\pi])
if its p.d.f. is
f(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}
for any
x\in[0,2\pi]
and
f(x)=0
otherwise.

5)

Exponential r.v.

\sim\mathscr{E}(\lambda)
if its p.d.f. is
f(x)=\lambda e^{-\lambda x}
for any
x\geq 0
and
f(x)=0

for any
x<0
.

Remark 1.6
.

The following simple
properties will be used repeatedly in the next sections.

(i)

If
a^{\omega}\sim{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbb{R}}(0,\sigma^{2})
then
\lambda a^{\omega}\sim{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbb{R}}(0,\lambda^{2}\sigma^{2})
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. Furthermore, the following tail estimate holds:

{\mathbb{P}}(a^{\omega}>z)\sim\frac{\sigma}{z\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{z^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ as }z\to\infty\,.

(1.43)

(ii)

If
a_{1}^{\omega}\sim{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbb{R}}(0,\sigma_{1}^{2})
and
a_{2}^{\omega}\sim{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbb{R}}(0,\sigma_{2}^{2})

are
independent
real Gaussian random variables, then the sum

a_{1}^{\omega}+a_{2}^{\omega}\sim{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbb{R}}(0,\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2})
is a real Gaussian r.v.

(iii)

If
\zeta^{\omega}\sim{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbb{C}}(0,\sigma^{2})
then
\mathbb{E}[\zeta^{\omega}]=0
and
\mathbb{E}[|\zeta^{\omega}|^{2}]=\sigma^{2}
.
For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
,

\lambda\zeta^{\omega}\sim{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbb{C}}(0,|\lambda|^{2}\sigma^{2})
and

\zeta^{\omega}=|\zeta^{\omega}|e^{{\rm i}\theta^{\omega}}

where the modulus

|\zeta^{\omega}|
is a Rayleigh r.v.
\sim\mathscr{R}\big(\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{2}}\big)
, and the phase
\theta^{\omega}
is a uniform r.v.

\sim\mathscr{U}([0,2\pi])
.

(iv)

If
R^{\omega}\sim\mathscr{R}(\sigma)
then
for any
\lambda>0
,
\lambda R^{\omega}\sim\mathscr{R}(\lambda\sigma)
.

(v)

If
X^{\omega}\sim{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbb{C}}(0,\sigma^{2})

then
|X^{\omega}|^{2}\sim\mathscr{E}(1/\sigma^{2})
.

(vi)

If

X^{\omega}
is
\sim\mathscr{E}(\lambda)

then
\alpha X^{\omega}
is

\sim\mathscr{E}(\lambda/\alpha)
.

Part I
Deterministic pure gravity deep water waves

In this part we present the deterministic well posedness theory of pure water waves equations in deep water.
For initial data in Sobolev spaces the local Cauchy theory was developed first by S. Wu
[
68
]
using a Lagrangian formulation of the equations and by
Lannes
[
51
]
and then Alazard-Burq-Zuily
[
1
]
in an Eulerian framework, i.e. for system (
1.5
), expressing the problem in terms of a “good unknown”

\omega
, instead of
\psi
, and
a paralinearization of the Dirichlet-Neuman
operator.
We also mention the more
geometric approach developed in
[
63
]
to study free boundary value problems.

Longer lifespans follow provided the
initial datum is sufficiently regular and small, as first proved in
[
5
]
via a paradifferential Birkhoff normal form approach, result extended to more general initial data in
[
69
,
26
]
.
These works
ultimately descend from the fact that the pure gravity water waves equations in deep water are
integrable
up to quartic vector fields, as formally guessed in
[
72
]
and rigorously proved in
[
5
]
. This allows to control the evolution of sufficiently smooth and small initial data of size
\varepsilon
, for times of order
\varepsilon^{-3}
, as stated in Theorem
2.6
below, which is actually a consequence of the Birkhoff normal form Theorem

2.4
, the energy estimates of Corollary
2.5
and the local existence result in
[
1
]
.

We prove Theorem
2.4
in Appendix
A
.
The difference of Theorem
2.4
with respect to
[
5
]
is that the normal form transformation
\mathfrak{B}(u)
that we construct satisfies the Lipschitz properties
(
2.14
), (
2.20
). Such properties are necessary in the probabilistic approach developed in
Part

II
.
In Theorem

2.8
below we collect

all
the deterministic information
on the water waves flow required for the probabilistic proof of the
Large deviation
3.1
.
In this way a reader may pass to
Part

II
, skipping the (more technical) Appendix
A
.

We first report basic definitions and
properties
of paradifferential calculus.

Paradifferential operators.

Paradifferential calculus
can be seen as a nonlinear version of pseudo-differential calculus for
symbols with finite regularity.

Definition 2.1
.

(Symbols)
Given
m\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote by
\Gamma^{m}_{L^{\infty}}
the space of
functions
a:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
,
(x,\xi)\mapsto a(x,\xi)
,
which are
C^{\infty}
with respect to
\xi
and such that, for any
\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
there exists a constant
C_{\beta}>0
such that

\big\|{\partial}_{\xi}^{\beta}\,a(\cdot,\xi)\big\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C_{\beta}\,\langle\xi\rangle^{m-\beta},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\xi\in\mathbb{R}\,.

We endow
\Gamma^{m}_{L^{\infty}}
with the family of norms defined, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, by

\left|a\right|_{m,{L^{\infty}},n}:=\max_{0\leq\beta\leq n}\,\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\,\big\|\langle\xi\rangle^{-m+\beta}\,{\partial}_{\xi}^{\beta}a(\cdot,\xi)\big\|_{L^{\infty}}\,.

(2.1)

Paradifferential operators are defined as pseudo-differential operators with a suitable regularized symbol.
Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and consider a

C^{\infty}
, even cut-off function
\chi\colon\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
such that

\chi(\xi)=\begin{cases}1&\mbox{ if }|\xi|\leq 1.1\\
0&\mbox{ if }|\xi|\geq 1.9\,,\end{cases}\hskip 20.00003pt\chi_{\epsilon}(\xi):=\chi\left(\frac{\xi}{\epsilon}\right)\,.

(2.2)

Definition 2.2
.

(Bony-Weyl paradifferential operator)

Given a symbol
a\in\Gamma^{m}_{L^{\infty}}
, we define the

Bony-Weyl paradifferential operator

{\rm Op}^{BW}{(a)}
that acts on a periodic function
u(x)

as

\left({\rm Op}^{BW}{(a)}[u]\right)(x)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{a}\Big(j-k,\frac{j+k}{2}\Big)\,\chi_{\epsilon}\Big(\frac{j-k}{\langle j+k\rangle}\Big)\,u_{k}\Big)e^{{\rm i}jx}\,.

If the symbol
a(x)
is independent of
\xi

the operator
{\rm Op}^{BW}{(a)}
is called a
paraproduct
.

It is convenient to use the Weyl quantization
for several reasons. In particular, it ensures that
a paradifferential operator is
self-adjoint if and only if
the symbol
a(x,\xi)
is real.

We remark that the operator

{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(a)
acts on homogeneous spaces of
2\pi
-periodic functions.
Furthermore for a symbol
a(\xi)
independent of
x
, the paradifferential operator

{\rm Op}^{BW}{(a)}=a(D)
reduces to a Fourier multiplier operator.
We also note that paradifferential operators are defined up to smoothing remainders, i.e. we can take different cut-off functions in (
2.2
).

The following basic property about the action of a paradifferential operator (see e.g.
[
6
]
) is often used.

Proposition 2.3
.

(Continuity)

Let
a
be a symbol in
\Gamma^{m}_{L^{\infty}}
with
m\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then
{\rm Op}^{BW}\!\left(a\right)
extends to a bounded operator

\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T})\to\dot{H}^{s-m}(\mathbb{T})
for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
and, for any
u\in\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T})
,

\left\lVert{\rm Op}^{BW}\!\left(a\right)u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s-m}}\lesssim\,\left|a\right|_{m,L^{\infty},4}\,\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\,.

(2.3)

For a paraproduct, the linear map

L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})\mapsto{\cal L}({\dot{H}}^{s})
,
a\mapsto{\rm Op}^{BW}\!\left(a(x)\right)
, taking values in the bounded linear operators of

{\dot{H}}^{s}(\mathbb{T})
satisfies
\left\lVert{\rm Op}^{BW}\!\left(a(x)\right)\right\rVert_{{\cal L}({\dot{H}}^{s})}\lesssim\|a\|_{L^{\infty}}
.

In
Section

A.1
we shall introduce
more refined classes of paradifferential symbols and smoothing operators which take into account their Taylor expansion in
(\eta,\psi)
at
(0,0)
, cf.
[
3
]
. This is needed in Appendix
A
for the proof of the normal form
2.4
.

Birkhoff normal form and long time existence results.

We now present the novel Birkhoff normal form Theorem
2.4
and some of its consequences.

We denote
the horizontal and vertical components of the velocity field at the free interface

\displaystyle V:=V(\eta,\psi):=({\partial}_{x}\Phi)(x,\eta(x))=\psi_{x}-\eta_{x}B\,,

(2.4)

\displaystyle B:=B(\eta,\psi):=({\partial}_{y}\Phi)(x,\eta(x))=\frac{G(\eta)\psi+\eta_{x}\psi_{x}}{1+\eta_{x}^{2}}\,,

(2.5)

and we introduce the “good unknown” of Alinhac

\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\upomega\end{pmatrix}={\mathcal{G}}(\eta,\psi):=\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\psi-{\rm Op}^{BW}\!\left(B\right)\eta\end{pmatrix}\,.

(2.6)

Note that
V,B
and

\upomega
, are linear functions of
\psi
.
The variables

(\eta,\upomega)
are introduced because,
as noted in
[
51
,
1
]
, they transform
the water waves equations into
a quasi-linear hyperbolic system, for which
Sobolev energy estimates are available,
thus guaranteeing the local existence of solutions.

For future reference we mention that,
by (
1.11
)
and the algebra properties of Sobolev spaces, for any
{\mathtt{s}}>\frac{7}{2}
, for some
r>0
small enough,
the maps in (
2.4
) and (
2.5
),

(\eta,\psi)\mapsto V(\eta,\psi)\,,(\eta,\psi)\mapsto B(\eta,\psi)\,,\hskip 10.00002ptB^{{\mathtt{s}}}(r)\times\dot{H}^{{\mathtt{s}}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\to H^{{\mathtt{s}}-1}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{are {\it analytic}}\,,

(2.7)

as well as the good-unknown map in (
2.6
),

(\eta,\psi)\mapsto{\cal G}(\eta,\psi)=(\eta,\upomega(\eta,\psi))\,\hskip 10.00002ptB^{{\mathtt{s}}}(r)\times\dot{H}^{{\mathtt{s}}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\to H^{{\mathtt{s}}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\times{\dot{H}}^{{\mathtt{s}}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{is {\it analytic}}\,,

(2.8)

using (
2.7
) and the last property stated in
2.3
.

We finally define the complex scalar variable

u:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}|D|^{-\frac{1}{4}}\eta+\frac{{\rm i}}{\sqrt{2}}|D|^{\frac{1}{4}}\upomega\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(t,x)\,{\rm d}x=0\,,

(2.9)

that is well defined since
\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta(t,\cdot)\,dx=0

is preserved by the flow of (
1.5
).
Expressing in (
2.9
) the good unknown

\upomega
as a function of
(\eta,\psi)

by (
2.6
), by (
2.8
) we deduce that,
for any
{\mathtt{s}}>\frac{7}{2}
, the map

(\eta,\psi)\mapsto u(\eta,\psi)\,,\hskip 10.00002ptB^{{\mathtt{s}}}(r)\times\dot{H}^{{\mathtt{s}}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\to H_{0}^{{\mathtt{s}}-\frac{1}{4}}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{is {\it analytic}}\,.

(2.10)

Finally note that the difference of the complex
variables
u
and
\zeta
, defined respectively in (
2.9
) and (
1.18
), is

u-\zeta=\frac{{\rm i}}{\sqrt{2}}|D|^{\frac{1}{4}}(\upomega-\psi)=-\frac{{\rm i}}{\sqrt{2}}|D|^{\frac{1}{4}}{\rm Op}^{BW}\!\left(B\right)\eta\,.

(2.11)

We now state the
normal form result for small amplitude pure gravity
water waves in deep water.

Theorem 2.4
.

(Birkhoff normal form with Lipschitz map)

Let
u(t)
be defined as in (
2.9
), with
\upomega
as in (
2.6
), for
(\eta,\psi)
solution of (
1.5
)
satisfying

(\eta,\psi)\in C^{0}\left([-T,T];H^{N+\frac{1}{4}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\times H^{N+\frac{1}{4}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\right)\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta(0,x){\rm d}x=0\,.

There exist
N\gg K{\gg s_{0}}\gg 1
and
0<\overline{{\epsilon}}\ll 1
, such that, if

\sup_{t\in[-T,T]}\left\lVert u(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{K,N}\ \leq\overline{{\epsilon}}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\left\lVert u(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{K,N}:=\sum_{k=0}^{K}\|\partial_{t}^{k}u(t)\|_{\dot{H}^{N-k}}\,,

(2.12)

then there exists a bounded
and linearly invertible transformation
\mathfrak{B}=\mathfrak{B}(u)
of
\dot{H}^{N}
, which depends (nonlinearly) on
u
,
and a constant
C:=C(N)>0

such that for any
{\mathtt{s}}\in[s_{0},N]

\displaystyle{\|\mathfrak{B}(u)\|}_{\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{{\mathtt{s}}},\dot{H}^{{\mathtt{s}}})}+{\|(\mathfrak{B}(u))^{-1}\|}_{\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{{\mathtt{s}}},\dot{H}^{{\mathtt{s}}})}\leq 1+C_{N}{\|u\|}_{\dot{H}^{{\mathtt{s}}}}\,,

(2.13)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathfrak{B}(u)-{\rm Id}\right\rVert_{{\mathcal{L}}(\dot{H}^{{\mathtt{s}}+1},\dot{H}^{{\mathtt{s}}})}\leq C_{\mathtt{s}}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{{\mathtt{s}}}}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\left\lVert\mathfrak{B}(u)^{-1}-{\rm Id}\right\rVert_{{\mathcal{L}}(\dot{H}^{{\mathtt{s}}+1},\dot{H}^{{\mathtt{s}}})}\leq C_{\mathtt{s}}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{{\mathtt{s}}}}\,,

(2.14)

and the variable

z:=\mathfrak{B}(u)u

satisfies the equation

{\partial}_{t}z=-{\rm i}{\partial}_{\overline{z}}H_{ZD}(z,\overline{z})+{\mathcal{X}}^{+}_{\geq 4}

(2.15)

where:

(1)

(integrable Birkhoff normal form)
the Hamiltonian
H_{ZD}
has the form

\displaystyle H_{ZD}=H^{(2)}_{ZD}+H^{(4)}_{ZD}\,,\hskip 20.00003ptH^{(2)}_{ZD}(z,\overline{z}):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\big||D|^{\frac{1}{4}}z\big|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\,,

(2.16)

with

\displaystyle H^{(4)}_{ZD}(z,\overline{z})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}|k|^{3}\big(|z_{k}|^{4}-2|z_{k}|^{2}|z_{-k}|^{2}\big)

(2.17)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\pi}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z},\,{\textup{sign}}(k_{1})={\textup{sign}}(k_{2})\\
|k_{2}|<|k_{1}|\end{subarray}}|k_{1}||k_{2}|^{2}\big(-|z_{-k_{1}}|^{2}|z_{k_{2}}|^{2}+|z_{k_{1}}|^{2}|z_{k_{2}}|^{2}\big)

where
z_{k}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}z(x)e^{-{\rm i}kx}dx
denotes the
k
-th Fourier coefficient of the function
z(x)
.

(2)

(estimate of the remainder)

{\mathcal{X}}^{+}_{\geq 4}:={\mathcal{X}}^{+}_{\geq 4}(u,\overline{u},z,\overline{z})

is a quartic nonlinear term satisfying,
for some
C:=C(N)>0
, the “energy estimate”

{\rm Re}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|D|^{N}{\mathcal{X}}^{+}_{\geq 4}\cdot\overline{|D|^{N}z}\,{\rm d}x\leq C\|u\|_{\dot{H}^{N}}^{5}

(2.18)

and

\|{\mathcal{X}}_{\geq 4}^{+}\|_{\dot{H}^{1}}\leq C\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}}}^{4}\,.

(2.19)

(3)

(Lipschitz property)

for any
{\mathtt{s}}\in[s_{0},N-1]
, there is
r:=r({\mathtt{s}})>0
,
C_{{\mathtt{s}},r}>0
such that for any
u_{i}\in\dot{H}^{{\mathtt{s}}}
with
\left\lVert u_{i}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{{\mathtt{s}}}}\leq r
,
i=1,2
, one has

\left\lVert\mathfrak{B}(u_{1})u_{1}-\mathfrak{B}(u_{2})u_{2}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{{\mathtt{s}}}}\leq C_{{\mathtt{s}},r}\left\lVert u_{1}-u_{2}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{{\mathtt{s}}}}(1+\left\lVert u_{1}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{{\mathtt{s}}+1}}+\left\lVert u_{2}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{{\mathtt{s}}+1}})\,.

(2.20)

In view of (
2.13
),
the norms of the solution
u(t)
and the normal form variable
z(t)
are equivalent

\left\lVert u(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{{\mathtt{s}}}}\sim\left\lVert z(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{{\mathtt{s}}}}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall{\mathtt{s}}\in[s_{0},N]\,.

(2.21)

In order to achieve the new Lipschitz property
(
2.20
)
we construct the normal form transformation
\mathfrak{B}(u)u
by a variant of the procedure in
[
5
]
.
The detailed proof is postponed
to
Appendix

A
.

We now state a series of consequences of Theorem
2.4
.
First note that system (
2.15
) writes explicitly in Fourier as

\dot{z}_{k}=-{\rm i}{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(I(z))\,z_{k}+({\mathcal{X}}^{+}_{\geq 4})_{k}\ ,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}\ ,

(2.22)

where
I(z)
is the “action vector”

I(z):=(I_{k}(z))_{k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\ ,\hskip 10.00002ptI_{k}(z):=(|z_{k}|^{2})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\,,

(2.23)

and

{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(I)
denotes the
k
-th “nonlinear frequency”

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(I):=

\displaystyle|k|^{\frac{1}{2}}-\frac{1}{\pi}\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|k_{4}|<|k|,\\
-{\textup{sign}}(k)={\textup{sign}}(k_{4})\end{subarray}}\!\!\!|k||k_{4}|^{2}I_{k_{4}}+\frac{1}{\pi}\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|k_{4}|<|k|,\\
{\textup{sign}}(k)={\textup{sign}}(k_{4})\end{subarray}}\!\!\!|k||k_{4}|^{2}I_{k_{4}}

(2.24)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2\pi}|k|^{3}\big(I_{k}-2I_{-k}\big)-\frac{1}{\pi}\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|k|<|k_{1}|,\\
{\textup{sign}}(k_{1})={\textup{sign}}(k)\end{subarray}}\!\!\!|k_{1}||k|^{2}\big(I_{-k_{1}}-I_{k_{1}}\big)\ .

We shall use that for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
the map

{\mathcal{F}}L^{2,1}\to\mathbb{R}\ ,\hskip 10.00002ptI:=(I_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\mapsto{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(I)\,,

is Lipschitz with the quantitative estimate: there exists
C>0
such that

{\left|{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(I)-{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(J)\right|\leq C|k|\left\lVert I-J\right\rVert_{{\mathcal{F}}L^{2,1}}}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\forall k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}\,,

(2.25)

where
\left\lVert I\right\rVert_{{\mathcal{F}}L^{2,1}}=\sum_{k\neq 0}k^{2}|I_{k}|=\sum_{k\neq 0}k^{2}|z_{k}|^{2}
, cf. (
1.42
).

Since each nonlinear frequency
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(I)
in (
2.24
) is real,
each action
I_{k}(z):=|z_{k}|^{2}
is a first integral of the cubic Hamiltonian Birkhoff normal form
system

\dot{z}_{k}=-{\rm i}{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(I(z))\,z_{k}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}\,,

(2.26)

obtained by neglecting
the quartic terms
({\mathcal{X}}^{+}_{\geq 4})_{k}
in (
2.22
).
This directly implies the following energy estimate.

Corollary 2.5
.

(Energy estimate)
With the same assumptions and notations of Theorem
2.4
, there exists
C=C(N)>0
such that the solution
z(t)
of (
2.15
) satisfies

\left\lVert z(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{N}}^{2}\leq\left\lVert z(0)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{N}}^{2}+C\,|t|\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\left\lVert u(\tau)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{N}}^{5}\ ,\hskip 20.00003pt\forall|t|\leq T\,,

(2.27)

and the vector of actions

I(t):=(I_{k}(z(t)))_{k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}
in (
2.23
)
satisfies

\left\lVert I(t)-I(0)\right\rVert_{{{\mathcal{F}}L^{2,1}}}\leq C|t|\,\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\left\lVert u(\tau)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}}}^{5}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\forall|t|\leq T\ .

(2.28)

Proof.

The energy estimate (
2.27
) follows because

\frac{d}{dt}\left\lVert z(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{N}}^{2}=2{\rm Re}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|D|^{N}{\mathcal{X}}^{+}_{\geq 4}\cdot\overline{|D|^{N}z}\,{\rm d}x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{theoBirR}}}{{\leq}}C\|u\|_{\dot{H}^{N}}^{5}\,.

Estimate (
2.28
) follows because
\frac{d}{dt}I_{k}(t)=2{\rm Re}((\mathcal{X}_{\geq 4}^{+})_{k}\overline{z}_{k})

for any
k
and so

\displaystyle\|\dot{I}\|_{{{\mathcal{F}}L^{2,1}}}\leq 2\sum_{k\neq 0}k^{2}|[\mathcal{X}_{\geq 4}^{+}]_{k}|\,|z_{k}(t)|\lesssim\left\lVert z(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1}}\,\left\lVert\mathcal{X}_{\geq 4}^{+}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1}}\lesssim_{N}\left\lVert u(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}}}^{5}

by (
2.19
) and the equivalence (
2.21
) of the norms

\left\lVert z(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}}}\sim\left\lVert u(t)\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}}}
.
∎

The long-time existence of solutions up
to times
t\sim\varepsilon^{-3}
, as stated in Theorem
2.6
, is a direct
consequence of the energy estimate
(
2.27
)
and
the local existence result in
[
1
]
. The latter
proved that, if
the initial data
(\eta,\psi,V,B)_{|t=0}
belong to the Banach space

X^{s}:=H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}_{0}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\times\dot{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})

(2.29)

with
s>\frac{3}{2}
, endowed with norm

\|(\eta,\psi,V,B)\|_{X^{s}}:=\|\eta\|_{H_{0}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}+\|\psi\|_{\dot{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}+\|V\|_{H^{s}}+\|B\|_{H^{s}}\,,

(2.30)

then there exists a time
T>0
and a unique solution

(\eta,\psi,V,B)\in C^{0}([-T,T],X^{s})
of (
1.5
).

Theorem 2.6
.

(Long time existence
[
5
, Theorem 1.2]
)

There exists
s_{0}>0
such that,
for all
s\geq s_{0}
, there is
{\epsilon}_{0}>0
such that,
for any initial data
(\eta_{0},\psi_{0})
satisfying

\|(\eta_{0},\psi_{0},V_{0},B_{0})\|_{X^{s}}\leq{\epsilon}\leq{\epsilon}_{0}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta_{0}(x){\rm d}x=0\,,

(2.31)

where
V_{0}:=V(\eta_{0},\psi_{0})
,
B_{0}:=B(\eta_{0},\psi_{0})
are defined by
(
2.4
)-(
2.5
), the following holds: there exist constants
c>0
,
C>0
and a unique classical solution

(\eta,\psi,V,B)\in C^{0}([-T_{\epsilon},T_{\epsilon}],X^{s})

(2.32)

of the water waves system (
1.5
)
with initial condition
(\eta,\psi)(0)=(\eta_{0},\psi_{0})

with

T_{\epsilon}\geq c{\epsilon}^{-3}\,,

(2.33)

satisfying

\sup_{[-T_{\epsilon},T_{\epsilon}]}\big(\|(\eta,\psi)\|_{H^{s}\times H^{s}}+\|(V,B)\|_{H^{s-1}\times H^{s-1}}\big)\leq{C}{\epsilon}\,

(2.34)

and

\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta(t,x){\rm d}x=0\,\ \ \ \forall t\in[-T_{\epsilon},T_{\epsilon}]\ .
