Deterministic nonlinear phase operator

\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
).

Let
{\mathtt{N}}\in\mathbb{N}
.
Fix

\delta\in(0,1)
and
s\geq N+\frac{5}{4}
.
For any
{\mathtt{R}}>0
, any
0<\kappa\ll 1-\delta
, there exists
\varepsilon_{0}:=\varepsilon_{0}({\mathtt{R}},\kappa)>0
and

C_{2}>0

such that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
,
for any
|t|\leq T_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{-3(1-\delta)+\kappa}
,
for any
0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}
,
the map
{\widetilde{{\mathcal{T}}}}_{j}
in (
5.30
) is well defined,

|\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(v,\vec{\phi})|\leq|t|\,\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}|{\mathcal{L}}_{j}(I(z(\tau;v,\vec{\phi})))|\leq C_{2}|t|{\mathtt{N}}\,,

(5.32)

and satisfies the Lipschitz property,
for any
(v,\vec{\phi})
,
(\underline{v},\underline{\vec{\phi}})
in

{\mathcal{Y}}(\varepsilon,\delta,{\mathtt{R}},s,{\mathtt{N}})\times\mathbb{R}^{2{\mathtt{N}}}
,

\left|\widetilde{{\mathcal{T}}}_{j}(v,\vec{\phi})-\widetilde{{\mathcal{T}}}_{j}(\underline{v},\underline{\vec{\phi}})\right|\leq C_{2}{\mathtt{N}}\,{\mathtt{R}}\,\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\,e^{C_{\rm lip}|t|}\left({\mathtt{R}}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\,\|\vec{\phi}-\underline{\vec{\phi}}\|_{\ell^{\infty}}+\varepsilon\,\left\lVert v-\underline{v}\right\rVert_{{\mathcal{Y}}}\right)

(5.33)

where
C_{\rm lip}
is the positive constant in
(
2.40
).

Proof.

For any

(v,\vec{\phi})\in{\mathcal{Y}}(\varepsilon,\delta,{\mathtt{R}},s,{\mathtt{N}})\times\mathbb{R}^{2{\mathtt{N}}}

(recall (
5.29
)), the initial data

({\widetilde{\eta}}_{0},{\widetilde{\psi}}_{0})(\cdot;v,\vec{\phi})
in (
5.31
)
belong to the ball
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}(\varepsilon,\delta,{\mathtt{R}},s)
in
(
1.30
). Thus by Theorem
2.8

the function
z(\tau;v,\vec{\phi})

in (
5.30
) is well defined for any
|t|\leq T_{\varepsilon}
, as well as the operator
{\widetilde{{\mathcal{T}}}}_{j}
in (
5.30
),
and
the estimate (
5.32
) follows by

|\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(v,\vec{\phi})|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{wtT}}}{{\leq}}|t|\,\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}|{\mathcal{L}}_{j}(I(z(\tau;v,\vec{\phi})))|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{HCS1},\eqref{z.control}}}{{\lesssim}}\,|t|\,{\mathtt{N}}(1+{\mathtt{R}}^{2}\varepsilon^{2-2\delta})\leq C_{1}|t|{\mathtt{N}}\,.

For any
(v,\vec{\phi}),(\underline{v},\vec{\underline{\phi}})
in
{\mathcal{Y}}(\varepsilon,\delta,{\mathtt{R}},s,{\mathtt{N}})\times\mathbb{R}^{2{\mathtt{N}}}
denote
z(\tau):=\Upsilon^{\tau}(({\widetilde{\eta}}_{0},{\widetilde{\psi}}_{0})(\cdot;v,\vec{\phi}))
and

\underline{z}(\tau):=\Upsilon^{\tau}(({\widetilde{\eta}}_{0},{\widetilde{\psi}}_{0})(\cdot;\underline{v},\vec{\underline{\phi}}))
.
The data in
(
5.31
) satisfy
the Lipschitz property

\big\|({\widetilde{\eta}}_{0},{\widetilde{\psi}}_{0})(\cdot;v,\vec{\phi})-({\widetilde{\eta}}_{0},{\widetilde{\psi}}_{0})(\cdot;\underline{v},\vec{\underline{\phi}})\big\|_{{H}_{0}^{s}\times\dot{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}\lesssim\varepsilon\,\left\lVert v-\underline{v}\right\rVert_{{\mathcal{Y}}}+{\mathtt{R}}\,\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\,\|\vec{\phi}-\underline{\vec{\phi}}\|_{\ell^{\infty}}\,.

(5.34)

The Lipschitz property of
I\mapsto{\mathcal{L}}_{j}(I)
in (
2.25
) and the one of
(\eta_{0},\psi_{0})\mapsto\Upsilon^{\tau}(\eta_{0},\psi_{0})
in
2.8
imply
that, for any
|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}
,

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{L}}_{j}\big(I(z(\tau))\big)-

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{j}\big(I(\underline{z}(\tau))\big)|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{L_lips}}}{{\lesssim}}{\mathtt{N}}\left\lVert I(z(\tau))-I(\underline{z}(\tau))\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}L^{2,1}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{actions}}}{{\lesssim}}{\mathtt{N}}\big(\left\lVert z(\tau)\right\rVert_{H^{2}}+\left\lVert\underline{z}(\tau)\right\rVert_{H^{2}}\big)\,\left\lVert z(\tau)-\underline{z}(\tau)\right\rVert_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{z.control},\eqref{lip.z}}}{{\lesssim}}{\mathtt{N}}\,{\mathtt{R}}\,\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\,e^{C_{\rm lip}|\tau|}\,\left\lVert({\widetilde{\eta}}_{0},{\widetilde{\psi}}_{0})(\cdot;v,\vec{\phi})-({\widetilde{\eta}}_{0},{\widetilde{\psi}}_{0})(\cdot;\underline{v},\vec{\underline{\phi}})\right\rVert_{H_{0}^{s_{0}+2}\times\dot{H}^{s_{0}+2}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{iota.lip}}}{{\lesssim}}{\mathtt{N}}\,{\mathtt{R}}\,\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\,e^{C_{\rm lip}|\tau|}\,\left({\mathtt{R}}\,\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\,\big\|\vec{\phi}-\underline{\vec{\phi}}\big\|_{\ell^{\infty}}+\varepsilon\,\left\lVert v-\underline{v}\right\rVert_{{\mathcal{Y}}}\right)\,.

Integrating in
\tau
in (
5.30
) one gets the Lipschitz estimate (
5.33
).
∎

We introduce the map

\begin{split}\iota:{\mathcal{B}}_{0}(\varepsilon,\delta,{\mathtt{R}},s)\subset\mathbb{\Omega}&\longrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}}(\varepsilon,\delta,{\mathtt{R}},s,{\mathtt{N}})\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2{\mathtt{N}}}\times{H}^{s}_{0}\times\dot{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}\,,\quad\omega\longmapsto(\vec{R}^{\omega},\varepsilon^{-1}\Pi_{>{\mathtt{N}}}{\widetilde{\eta}}_{0}^{\omega},\varepsilon^{-1}\Pi_{>{\mathtt{N}}}{\widetilde{\psi}}_{0}^{\omega})\,,\end{split}

(5.35)

where

({\widetilde{\eta}}_{0}^{\omega},{\widetilde{\psi}}_{0}^{\omega})(x;\vec{\phi})

and
\vec{R}^{\omega}:=(R_{j}^{\omega})_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}}

are defined in (
5.13
).
Note that, by its very definition,
\iota
is
{\widetilde{{\mathcal{F}}}}
-measurable, since
{\widetilde{{\mathcal{F}}}}=\sigma(\iota)
, cf. (
5.18
).
The map
\iota
turns the deterministic datum (
5.31
) into the random datum (
5.13
), i.e.

({\widetilde{\eta}}_{0},{\widetilde{\psi}}_{0})(x;\iota(\omega),\vec{\phi})=({\widetilde{\eta}}_{0}^{\omega},{\widetilde{\psi}}_{0}^{\omega})(x;\vec{\phi})\,.

(5.36)

As a result

{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(\omega,\vec{\phi})={{\widetilde{{\mathcal{T}}}}}_{j}(\iota(\omega),\vec{\phi})\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall 0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}\,.

(5.37)

We now prove the first two
properties (
i
)-(
ii
) stated in
5.3
.

(
i
) It follows by (
5.37
) and (
5.33
)
with
v=\underline{v}=\iota(\omega)
.

(
ii
) In view of
(
5.37
) it suffices to prove the measurability of
{\widetilde{{\mathcal{T}}}}_{j}(\cdot,\vec{\phi})
and
\iota
separately. The operator
{\widetilde{{\mathcal{T}}}}_{j}(\cdot,\vec{\phi})
is Lipschitz by
5.6
, thus continuous. Moreover, the map
\iota
is weakly measurable. By the Pettis measurability theorem
1
1

1

A mapping from a measure space to a separable Banach space is weakly measurable if and only if it is strongly measurable.

[
60
]
, and by the separability of the space
H^{s}
, this mapping is also strongly measurable.
As a result, for each fixed
\vec{\phi}
, the map
{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(\cdot,\vec{\phi})\colon({\mathcal{B}}_{0}(\varepsilon,\delta,{\mathtt{R}},s),{\widetilde{{\mathcal{F}}}})\to(\mathbb{R}^{2{\mathtt{N}}},{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbb{R}^{2{\mathtt{N}}}))
is also measurable.

(
iii
) We now prove the
existence of a random
fixed point theorem of
{\mathcal{T}}
. In view of
(
5.37
) and (
5.32
),
provided
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small, we have

\max_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}}|{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(\omega,\vec{\phi})|\leq{\mathtt{N}}\,\varepsilon^{-3}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall|t|\leq T_{\varepsilon}<\varepsilon^{-3}\,.

(5.38)

Define the set
K:=[-{\mathtt{N}}\,\varepsilon^{-3},{\mathtt{N}}\,\varepsilon^{-3}]^{2{\mathtt{N}}}
.
For each
\omega
, the bound
(
5.38
) guarantees that
{\mathcal{T}}(\omega,\cdot):K\rightarrow K
and
5.3
-(
i
) that
each
{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(\omega,\cdot):K\rightarrow K
is continuous. By
5.3
-(
ii
), each
{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(\cdot,\phi):({\mathcal{B}}_{0}(\varepsilon,\delta,{\mathtt{R}},s),{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2{\mathtt{N}}}
is a random operator.
Then the random Brouwer fixed point
1.3
guarantees the existence of a random variable
\vec{\phi}^{\ast,\omega}
in
({\mathcal{B}}_{0}(\varepsilon,\delta,{\mathtt{R}},s),{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}},{\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}}})
satisfying (
5.20
).

Next, we extend the random variable
\vec{\phi}^{\ast,\omega}
to the full probability space
(\mathbb{\Omega},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{{\mathbb{P}}})
by setting
\vec{\phi}^{\ast,\omega}:=0
for any
\omega\in\mathbb{\Omega}\setminus{\mathcal{B}}_{0}(\varepsilon,\delta,{\mathtt{R}},s)
. Note that, with this definition,
\vec{\phi}^{\ast,\omega}
is still
{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}
-measurable.

Finally, we prove the properties
(a)
-
(c)

of the fixed point in
5.3
.

(
a
) The map
F:\omega\mapsto(\vec{\phi}^{\omega},\vec{\phi}^{\ast,\omega})
is
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable and since

G:\mathbb{R}^{{\mathtt{N}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{{\mathtt{N}}}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

(a,b)\mapsto\left\lVert a-b\right\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}
,
is continuous, the composed map
G\circ F
is measurable, as well as
(G\circ F)^{-1}([0,r))
for any
r>0
. Consequently, the set
\mathcal{N}(\alpha)
in (
5.21
) is measurable. Its measure is
(2\alpha)^{2{\mathtt{N}}}
as follows by item
(c)
with
{\mathcal{A}}=\mathbb{\Omega}
.

(
b
) For any
\omega\in\mathcal{N}(\alpha)\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{0}(\varepsilon,\delta,{\mathtt{R}},s)
, for any

|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-3(1-\delta)+\kappa}
, we have, for any
0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}
,

\begin{split}\big|\phi_{j}^{\omega}-{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(\omega,\vec{\phi}^{\omega})\big|&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:T_fixedp}}}{{\leq}}\big|\phi_{j}-\phi_{j}^{\ast,\omega}\big|+\big|{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(\omega,\vec{\phi}^{\ast,\omega})-{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(\omega,\vec{\phi}^{\omega})\big|\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:nhood_fixedp},\eqref{eq:bilipschitz}}}{{\leq}}\alpha+\,C_{2}\,{\mathtt{N}}{\mathtt{R}}^{2}\,\varepsilon^{2(1-\delta)}\,e^{C_{\mathrm{lip}}|t|}\,\big\|\vec{\phi}^{\ast,\omega}-\vec{\phi}^{\omega}\big\|_{\ell^{\infty}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:nhood_fixedp}}}{{\leq}}2\alpha\,\,{\mathtt{N}}\,e^{C_{\mathrm{lip}}\varepsilon^{-3(1-\delta)}}\end{split}

for
\varepsilon
small.

(
c
) Using the standard properties of conditional expectation in
1.4
,
for any
{\mathcal{A}}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
we have

{\mathbb{P}}\left({\mathcal{A}}\cap\mathcal{N}(\alpha)\right)=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}}\,\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{N}(\alpha)}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}}\,\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{N}(\alpha)}\,|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\right]\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{N}(\alpha)}\,|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\right]\right]\,.

(5.39)

Consider now the random variable

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{N}(\alpha)}\,|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\big]
.
We wish to compute it explicitly using
Lemma
4.2
with
{\mathcal{G}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
,
X=\vec{\phi}^{\omega}
,
Y=\vec{\phi}^{\ast,\omega}
and
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{2{\mathtt{N}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{2{\mathtt{N}}}\to\mathbb{R}
is the bounded function defined by

f(x,y):=\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(y;\alpha)}(x)\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(y;\alpha):=\left\{\vec{\phi}\in\mathbb{R}^{2{\mathtt{N}}}\ \big|\ \|\vec{\phi}-y\|_{\ell^{\infty}}<\alpha\right\}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall y\in{\mathtt{R}}^{2{\mathtt{N}}}\,.

The assumptions are met since

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\subset{\mathcal{F}}
,
Y=\vec{\phi}^{\ast,\omega}
is
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable according to
5.3
-(
a
), and
the random variable
X=\vec{\phi}^{\omega}
is independent from
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
, see

5.4
.
Then
4.2
yields

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{N}(\alpha)}\,|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\right]=g(\vec{\phi}^{\ast,\omega})\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ with }\hskip 10.00002ptg(y):=\mathbb{E}\left[f(\vec{\phi}^{\omega},y)]\right]=\left(\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\right)^{2{\mathtt{N}}}\ .

Plugging into (
5.39
) proves (
5.23
).
The proof of
5.3
is complete.

5.3
The lower bound

We now prove the lower bound for (
1.26
) in
3.1

(ii)
.

Proposition 5.7

(
LDP: Lower-bound on optimal timescale).

There exists
\underline{s}>0

such that
for any
\lambda_{0}>0
,
\delta\in(\frac{3}{5},1)
, and
s\geq\underline{s}
there exists
{\mathtt{R}}_{0}>0
and for any
{\mathtt{R}}\geq{\mathtt{R}}_{0}
, any
0<\kappa\ll 1-\delta
, the following holds true. There exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
,
for any random initial data
(\eta_{0}^{\omega},\psi_{0}^{\omega})\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}(\varepsilon,\delta,{\mathtt{R}},s)
in (
1.30
),
the solution
(\eta(t,x),\psi(t,x))
of the water waves equations (
1.5
) exists up to times
|t|\leq T_{\varepsilon}:=\varepsilon^{-3(1-\delta)+\kappa}
, and satisfies
the lower bound

\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\varepsilon^{2\delta}\log{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\eta(t,x)\geq\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\right\}\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{0}(\varepsilon,\delta,{\mathtt{R}},s)\right)\geq-\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}}{2{\boldsymbol{\upsigma}}^{2}}\,,

(5.40)

with
{\boldsymbol{\upsigma}}
in (
1.16
).

Proof.

Let
s\geq\underline{s}:=N+\frac{5}{4}
(with
N
given by
2.4
) and
{\mathtt{R}}\geq{\mathtt{R}}_{0}
with
{\mathtt{R}}_{0}
defined in (
4.25
).
For any
\varepsilon>0

we choose

{\mathtt{N}}:={\mathtt{N}}_{\varepsilon}:=\lfloor\varepsilon^{-\gamma}\rfloor\in\mathbb{N}\ ,\hskip 10.00002pt0<\gamma<5\delta-3\,,

(5.41)

which is possible since
\delta>3/5
.
By
5.1
,
provided
\varepsilon
is small enough,
any initial datum
(\eta_{0}^{\omega},\psi_{0}^{\omega})\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}(\varepsilon,\delta,{\mathtt{R}},s)\equiv{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
gives rise to a water waves solution defined
for any
|t|\leq T_{\varepsilon}
, whose wave profile
is uniformly approximated by

\eta_{\rm app2}(t,x)
defined in (
5.1
), as stated in (
5.4
).
By the triangle inequality

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\eta(t,x)\geq\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\eta_{\rm app2}(t,x)-\left\lVert\eta(t)-\eta_{\mathrm{app2}}(t)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}

(5.42)

and
\eta(t,x)
becomes large
if the approximate profile

\eta_{\rm app2}(t,x)
does.
We define the event

\mathcal{C}_{\varepsilon}:=\left\{\left\lVert\eta(t)-\eta_{\mathrm{app2}}(t)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\geq\underline{{\mathtt{C}}}\,\lambda_{0}{\mathtt{R}}^{4}\,\,\varepsilon^{1-\delta+\kappa}\right\}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\underline{{\mathtt{C}}}:=2{{\mathtt{C}}_{2}}\lambda_{0}^{-1}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{with}\hskip 10.00002pt{\mathtt{C}}_{2}\mbox{ in }\eqref{upxi_error2}\,.

By
5.1
,
for any
|t|\leq T_{\varepsilon}
we have the inclusion

{\mathcal{B}}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{\varepsilon}^{c}
, hence

{\mathcal{B}}_{0}={\mathcal{B}}_{0}\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{\varepsilon}^{c}
, and
by (
5.42
)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}

\displaystyle\Big(\Big\{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\eta(t,x)\geq\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\Big\}\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{0}\Big)

\displaystyle\geq{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\Big\{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\eta_{\rm app2}(t,x)-\left\lVert\eta(t)-\eta_{\rm app2}(t)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\geq\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\Big\}\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{0}\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{\varepsilon}^{c}\Big)

\displaystyle\geq{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\Big\{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\eta_{\mathrm{app2}}(t,x)\geq\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}(1+\underline{{\mathtt{C}}}{\mathtt{R}}^{4}\,\varepsilon^{\kappa})\Big\}\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{0}\Big)

(5.43)

\displaystyle\geq{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\Big\{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\eta_{\mathrm{app2}}(t,x)\geq\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}(1+\underline{{\mathtt{C}}}{\mathtt{R}}^{4}\,\varepsilon^{\kappa})\Big\}\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{0}\cap\mathcal{N}(\alpha)\Big)

(5.44)

where

\mathcal{N}(\alpha)
is the measurable
set in (
5.21
) with

\alpha:=(2{\mathtt{N}}_{\varepsilon})^{-1}\,\varepsilon\,e^{-C_{\rm lip}\varepsilon^{-3(1-\delta)}}\ .

(5.45)

With this choice of
\alpha
, condition (
5.24
) is fulfilled and then we apply

5.5
(with
{\mathtt{N}}_{\varepsilon}
in (
5.41
))
to deduce that

(
5.44
)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{cor:eta.syn}}}{{\geq}}{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\Big\{\sum_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}_{\varepsilon}}c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}\,\geq\sqrt{\pi}\,\lambda_{0}\frac{\varepsilon^{-\delta}}{1-\varepsilon}(1+\underline{{\mathtt{C}}}{\mathtt{R}}^{4}\,\varepsilon^{\kappa}+C\lambda_{0}^{-1}\varepsilon^{\gamma(s-1)}{\mathtt{R}})\Big\}\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{0}\cap\mathcal{N}(\alpha)\Big)

\displaystyle\geq{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\underbrace{\Big\{\sum_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}_{\varepsilon}}c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}\,\geq\,\sqrt{\pi}\,\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{-\delta}(1+o_{\varepsilon}(1))\Big\}\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}_{=:{\mathcal{A}}}\cap\,\mathcal{N}(\alpha)\Big)\,.

(5.46)

The set
{\mathcal{A}}
depends only on the random variables
\big(\vec{R}^{\omega},\Pi_{>{\mathtt{N}}}{\widetilde{\eta}}_{0}^{\omega},\Pi_{>{\mathtt{N}}}{\widetilde{\psi}}_{0}^{\omega}\big)
and therefore is

{\widetilde{{\mathcal{F}}}}
-measurable (recall (
5.18
)). In addition we claim that

{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{A}})\geq\,\exp\Big(-\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}\,\varepsilon^{-2\delta}}{2{\boldsymbol{\upsigma}}^{2}}+o(\varepsilon^{-2\delta})\Big)\,.

(5.47)

Assuming this lower bound for the moment, let us conclude the proof of (
5.40
).
We give a lower bound on the right-hand side of (
5.46
).
By (
5.23
)
we have

{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{A}}\cap\mathcal{N}(\alpha))=\left(\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\right)^{2{\mathtt{N}}_{\varepsilon}}\,{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{A}})
.
By the choice of
{\mathtt{N}}_{\varepsilon}
in (
5.41
), of
\alpha
in (
5.45
) and that
0<\gamma<5\delta-3
(cf. (
5.41
)) we have

0\leq-2{\mathtt{N}}_{\varepsilon}\log(\alpha/\pi)={\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{-3(1-\delta)-\gamma})=o(\varepsilon^{-2\delta})\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\left(\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\right)^{2{\mathtt{N}}_{\varepsilon}}=\exp(-o(\varepsilon^{-2\delta}))\,.

(5.48)

Hence by (
5.48
) and (
5.47
) we deduce that

\displaystyle\eqref{1909:1645}\geq{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{A}}\cap\mathcal{N}(\alpha))

\displaystyle{\geq}\exp\Big(-\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}\,\varepsilon^{-2\delta}}{2{\boldsymbol{\upsigma}}^{2}}-o(\varepsilon^{-2\delta})\Big)\,.

(5.49)

Finally we conclude by (
5.46
)
and (
5.49
), that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\Big\{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\eta(t,x)\geq\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\Big\}\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{0}\Big)

\displaystyle\geq\exp\Big(-\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}\,\varepsilon^{-2\delta}}{2{\boldsymbol{\upsigma}}^{2}}-o(\varepsilon^{-2\delta})\Big)

and the desired bound (
5.40
) follows.

Proof of (
5.47
):

By the very definition of
{\mathcal{A}}
in (
5.46
), we estimate

{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{A}})\geq{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\Big\{\sum_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}_{\varepsilon}}c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}\,\geq\sqrt{\pi}\lambda_{0}\,\varepsilon^{-\delta}(1+o_{\varepsilon}(1))\Big\}\Big)-{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{c})

(5.50)

and we bound the two probabilities separately.

For any
{\mathtt{M}}\in\mathbb{N}
, for any
\varepsilon
small enough we have

{\mathtt{N}}_{\varepsilon}\geq{\mathtt{M}}
, and then

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\Big\{\sum_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}_{\varepsilon}}c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}\geq\sqrt{\pi}\lambda_{0}\,\varepsilon^{-\delta}(1+o_{\varepsilon}(1))\,\Big\}\Big)\geq{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\Big\{\sum_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{M}}}c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}\geq\sqrt{\pi}\lambda_{0}\,\varepsilon^{-\delta}(1+o_{\varepsilon}(1))\,\Big\}\Big)\,.

We apply Lemma
4.5
to the right hand side with a sequence
(c_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}
with
c_{j}=0
for any
|j|>{\mathtt{M}}
, getting

\displaystyle\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{2\delta}\log{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\Big\{\sum_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}_{\varepsilon}}c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}\geq\sqrt{\pi}\lambda_{0}\,\varepsilon^{-\delta}(1+o_{\varepsilon}(1))\,\Big\}\Big)\geq-\frac{\pi\,\lambda_{0}^{2}}{\sum_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{M}}}c_{j}^{2}}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall{\mathtt{M}}\in\mathbb{N}\,.

Then taking
{\mathtt{M}}\to\infty

and recalling (
1.16
) yields the lower bound

\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{2\delta}\log{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\Big\{\sum_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}_{\varepsilon}}c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}\geq\sqrt{\pi}\lambda_{0}(1+o_{\varepsilon}(1))\,\varepsilon^{-\delta}\,\Big\}\Big)\geq-\frac{2\,\lambda_{0}^{2}}{{\boldsymbol{\upsigma}}^{2}}\ .

(5.51)

By (
3.8
), the probability
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{c})\leq-\exp\left(-\frac{{\mathtt{R}}^{2}\,\varepsilon^{-2\delta}}{4\left\lVert\vec{c}\right\rVert_{h^{s}}^{2}}+1\right)
.
Taking
{\mathtt{R}}\geq{\mathtt{R}}_{0}>2\lambda_{0}\|\vec{c}\|_{h^{s}}{\boldsymbol{\upsigma}}^{-1}
(cf. (
4.25
)),
shows that
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{c})\leq\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\Big\{\sum_{0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{N}}_{\varepsilon}}c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}\,\geq\sqrt{\pi}\lambda_{0}\,\varepsilon^{-\delta}(1+o_{\varepsilon}(1))\Big\}\Big)
for
\varepsilon
small enough.
In view of (
5.50
), (
5.51
), the claimed estimate (
5.47
) follows.
∎

5.4
Dispersive focusing

In this section we prove
1.2
.
The upper bound in (
1.29
) follows by
1.1
since
{\mathbb{P}}(\mathfrak{R}^{t}(\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta})\cap\mathfrak{P}^{t}({\mathtt{M}},\varepsilon^{\frac{\kappa}{5}}))\leq{\mathbb{P}}(\mathfrak{R}^{t}(\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}))
.
To establish the lower bound in (
1.29
), we proceed as follows.
Approximating again
\eta(t,x)
with
\eta_{\rm app2}(t,x)
(defined in (
5.1
)) as we did to obtain the inequality in (
5.43
), we get

{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\mathfrak{R}^{t}(\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta})\cap\mathfrak{P}^{t}({\mathtt{M}},\varepsilon^{\frac{\kappa}{5}})\Big)\geq{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\mathcal{A}_{2}^{t}\left(\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}(1+o_{\varepsilon}(1))\right)\cap\mathfrak{P}^{t}({\mathtt{M}},\varepsilon^{\frac{\kappa}{5}})\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{0}\Big)

(5.52)

where
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}:={\mathcal{B}}_{0}(\varepsilon,\delta,{\mathtt{R}},s)
as in (
1.30
)-(
1.31
), and

\mathcal{A}_{2}^{t}(\lambda):=\Big\{\omega\in\mathbb{\Omega}\ \big|\ \sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\eta_{\mathrm{app2}}(t,x)\geq\lambda\Big\}.

(5.53)

Next we define the event

\mathcal{R}({\mathtt{M}},\varepsilon):=\Big\{\omega\in\mathbb{\Omega}\mid R_{k}^{\omega}\geq\varepsilon^{-\delta+\frac{\kappa}{2}}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall 0<|k|\leq{\mathtt{M}}\Big\}\,.

(5.54)

We claim that

(
i
)
\mathcal{R}({\mathtt{M}},\varepsilon)^{c}
is negligible for rogue waves, i.e.

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\varepsilon^{2\delta}\log{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\mathcal{A}_{2}^{t}\big(\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}(1+o_{\varepsilon}(1))\big)\cap\mathcal{R}({\mathtt{M}},\varepsilon)^{c}\Big)<-\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}}{2{\boldsymbol{\upsigma}}^{2}}\,.

(5.55)

(
ii
) Within the set
{\mathcal{N}}(\alpha)

defined in (
5.21
) and with
\alpha
in (
5.45
),
a lower bound on the moduli
R_{j}^{\omega}

implies the phases are synchronized, i.e.

\mathfrak{P}^{t}({\mathtt{M}},\varepsilon^{\frac{\kappa}{5}})\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{0}\supset\mathcal{N}(\alpha)\cap\mathcal{R}({\mathtt{M}},\varepsilon)\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{0}\,.

(5.56)

Assuming (
i
)-(
ii
), we can prove the sharp lower bound (
1.29
). Indeed

(
5.52
)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{0210:1455}}}{{\geq}}{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\mathcal{A}_{2}^{t}\left(\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}(1+o_{\varepsilon}(1))\right)\cap\mathcal{N}(\alpha)\cap\mathcal{R}({\mathtt{M}},\varepsilon)\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{0}\Big)

\displaystyle\geq{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\mathcal{A}_{2}^{t}\left(\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}(1+o_{\varepsilon}(1))\right)\cap\mathcal{N}(\alpha)\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{0}\Big)-{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\mathcal{A}_{2}^{t}\left(\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}(1+o_{\varepsilon}(1))\right)\cap\mathcal{R}({\mathtt{M}},\varepsilon)^{c}\Big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{1909:1726}}}{{\geq}}\exp\Big(-\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}\,\varepsilon^{-2\delta}}{2{\boldsymbol{\upsigma}}^{2}}-o(\varepsilon^{-2\delta})\Big)-{\mathbb{P}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{2}^{t}\left((\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}(1+o_{\varepsilon}(1))\right)\cap\mathcal{R}({\mathtt{M}},\varepsilon)^{c}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{0210:1452}}}{{\geq}}\exp\Big(-\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}\,\varepsilon^{-2\delta}}{2{\boldsymbol{\upsigma}}^{2}}-o(\varepsilon^{-2\delta})\Big)

proving (
1.29
). We now prove the claims (
i
) and (
ii
).

(
i
)
Proof of (
5.55
).

Recalling (
5.54
)
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\mathcal{A}_{2}^{t}\left(\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}(1+o_{\varepsilon}(1))\right)\cap\mathcal{R}({\mathtt{M}},\varepsilon)^{c}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{0<|k|\leq{\mathtt{M}}}{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\mathcal{A}_{2}^{t}\left(\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{1-\delta}(1+o_{\varepsilon}(1))\right)\cap\left\{R_{k}^{\omega}<\varepsilon^{-\delta+\frac{\kappa}{2}}\right\}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{defA2app},\eqref{eq:eta2notg}}}{{\leq}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{0<|k|\leq{\mathtt{M}}}\!\!\!{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\Big\{\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}\geq\sqrt{\pi}\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{-\delta}(1+o_{\varepsilon}(1))\Big\}\cap\left\{R_{k}^{\omega}<\varepsilon^{-\delta+\frac{\kappa}{2}}\right\}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\leq\sum_{0<|k|\leq{\mathtt{M}}}{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\Big\{\sum_{j\neq k}c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}\geq\sqrt{\pi}\lambda_{0}\varepsilon^{-\delta}(1+o_{\varepsilon}(1))\Big\}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:LDP_Rayleigh}}}{{\leq}}\sum_{0<|k|\leq{\mathtt{M}}}\exp\Big(-\frac{\pi\lambda_{0}^{2}}{\sum_{j\neq k}c_{j}^{2}}\,\varepsilon^{-2\delta}+o(\varepsilon^{-2\delta})\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\leq{\mathtt{M}}\,\exp\Big(-\frac{\pi\lambda_{0}^{2}}{\sum_{j\neq{\mathtt{M}}}c_{j}^{2}}\,\varepsilon^{-2\delta}+o(\varepsilon^{-2\delta})\Big)

(5.57)

where in the last step we used

\sum_{j\neq{\mathtt{M}}}c_{j}^{2}\geq{\sum_{j\neq k}c_{j}^{2}}
for any
0<|k|\leq{\mathtt{M}}
, since the
c_{j}
in (
1.14
) are decreasing.
Taking the logarithm in (
5.57
), multiplying by
\varepsilon^{2\delta}
, and taking the limsup as
\varepsilon\to 0
, yields (
5.55
), recalling also that
{\boldsymbol{\upsigma}}^{2}>\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{j\neq{\mathtt{M}}}c_{j}^{2}
by definition, see (
1.16
).

(
ii
)
Proof of (
5.56
).

Take
\omega\in\mathcal{N}(\alpha)\cap\mathcal{R}({\mathtt{M}},\varepsilon)\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
, cf.
(
5.21
), (
5.54
), (
1.31
). We wish to prove that
\omega\in\mathfrak{P}^{t}({\mathtt{M}},\varepsilon^{\frac{\kappa}{5}})
defined in
(
1.28
).
Expand in Fourier the
water waves solution with random initial data (
1.13
),

u(t,x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}u_{j}(t)e^{{\rm i}jx}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}|u_{j}(t)|e^{{\rm i}\theta_{j}(t)}e^{{\rm i}jx}\,,

provided by Theorem
2.8
.
Also recall that
by (
5.1
)-(
5.3
) the
j
-th
Fourier coefficient of the approximate solution
u_{\rm app2}(t,x)
is equal to

(u_{\rm app2})_{j}=\varepsilon c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}|j|^{-\frac{1}{4}}e^{{\rm i}\vartheta_{j}(t;\omega)}
. Then,
by the triangle inequality,
the continuous embedding

{\mathcal{F}}L^{\frac{1}{4},1}\hookrightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{T})

and
5.1
, we get that
for any
\omega\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
,
for any
|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-3(1-\delta)+\kappa}
,

\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\Big||j|^{\frac{1}{4}}|u_{j}(t)|-\underbrace{\varepsilon c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}}_{=|j|^{\frac{1}{4}}|(u_{\rm app2})_{j}|}\Big|\lesssim\left\lVert u(t,\cdot)-u_{\rm app2}(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{{\mathcal{F}}L^{\frac{1}{4},1}}\lesssim{\mathtt{R}}^{4}\varepsilon^{1-\delta+\kappa}\,.

(5.58)

Then the triangle inequality yields

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\!\!\!\!\varepsilon c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}\left|e^{{\rm i}\theta_{j}(t)}-e^{{\rm i}\vartheta_{j}(t;\omega)}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\Big(\left|\varepsilon c_{j}R_{j}^{\omega}-|j|^{\frac{1}{4}}|u_{j}(t)|\right|+\Big||j|^{\frac{1}{4}}\underbrace{|u_{j}(t)|e^{{\rm i}\theta_{j}(t)}}_{=u_{j}(t)}-|j|^{\frac{1}{4}}(u_{\rm app2})_{j}\Big|\Big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{flchec}}}{{\lesssim}}\left\lVert u(t,\cdot)-u_{\rm app2}(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{{\mathcal{F}}L^{\frac{1}{4},1}}\!\!\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{flchec}}}{{\lesssim}}{\mathtt{R}}^{4}\varepsilon^{1-\delta+\kappa}\,.

(5.59)

Exploiting that
\omega\in\mathcal{R}({\mathtt{M}},\varepsilon)
(cf. (
5.54
)) the estimate (
5.59
) implies that

c_{j}\,\big|e^{{\rm i}\theta_{j}(t)}-e^{{\rm i}\vartheta_{j}(t;\omega)}\big|\lesssim\varepsilon^{\kappa/2}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall 0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{M}}\,.

(5.60)

Finally since
\omega\in{\mathcal{N}}(\alpha)
,
with
\alpha
in (
5.45
),

5.5
yields
|\vartheta_{j}(t;\omega)|\leq\varepsilon
for any
0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{M}}
(for
\varepsilon
small enough). As a result, inserting in (
5.60
) we deduce

|e^{{\rm i}\theta_{j}(t)}-1|\lesssim\varepsilon^{\kappa/2}
for any
0<|j|\leq{\mathtt{M}}
, meaning that

\omega\in\mathfrak{P}^{t}({\mathtt{M}},\varepsilon^{\frac{\kappa}{5}})
for
\varepsilon
small enough
(recall (
1.28
)). This proves (
5.56
).
∎

Appendix A
Proof of the normal form theorem

In this section we prove Theorem
2.4
.
It is a variant of the
paradifferential Birkhoff normal form result in
[
5
]
. We first introduce paradifferential calculus following
[
3
,
5
]
, reporting only definitions and properties that will be used along the proof of
2.4
.

A.1
Paradifferential calculus

Given an interval
I=(-T,T)\subset\mathbb{R}

and
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
we define the space of functions

C_{*}^{K}(I,\dot{H}^{s}\left(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})\right):=\bigcap_{k=0}^{K}C^{k}\big(I,\dot{H}^{s-k}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})\big)

endowed with the norm

\sup_{t\in I}\|U(t,\cdot)\|_{K,s}\hskip 20.00003pt{\rm where}\hskip 20.00003pt\|U(t,\cdot)\|_{K,s}:={\mathop{\sum}}_{k=0}^{K}\|\partial_{t}^{k}U(t,\cdot)\|_{\dot{H}^{s-k}}\,,

and its subspace

C_{*\mathbb{R}}^{K}(I,\dot{H}^{s}\left(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})\right):=\Big\{U\in C_{*}^{K}(I,\dot{H}^{s}\left(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})\right)\ :\ U=\begin{pmatrix}u\\
\overline{u}\end{pmatrix}\Big\}\,.

The parameter
s
will denote the space Sobolev regularity of the water waves solution
U(t,\cdot)

and
K
its time regularity. Since
the gravity water waves vector field
loses
1
-derivative,
{\partial}_{t}^{k}U(t,\cdot)
will
belong to
{\dot{H}}^{s-k}
. We denote
\dot{H}^{s}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})

the subspaces of functions
U=\Big(\begin{smallmatrix}u\\
\overline{u}\end{smallmatrix}\Big)
of
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
.

Given
r>0
we denote the ball

B^{K}_{s,\mathbb{R}}(I;r):=\left\{U\in C_{*\mathbb{R}}^{K}(I,\dot{H}^{s}\left(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})\right)\colon\ \ \sup_{t\in I}\|U(t,\cdot)\|_{K,s}\leq r\right\}

(A.1)

and similarly by
B^{K}_{s}(I;r)

the ball of radius
r
in
C_{*}^{K}(I,\dot{H}^{s}\left(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})\right)
.

Given a linear operator
R(U)[\cdot]
acting on
\dot{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C})

we associate the linear operator

\overline{R}(U)[v]:=\overline{R(U)[\overline{v}]}
for all
v:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
.
We say that a matrix of operators acting on
\dot{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
is
real-to-real
, if it has the form

R(U)=\left(\begin{matrix}R_{1}(U)&R_{2}(U)\\
\overline{R_{2}}(U)&\overline{R_{1}}(U)\end{matrix}\right)\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\,U=\Big(\begin{smallmatrix}u\\
\overline{u}\end{smallmatrix}\Big)\,.

(A.2)

Similarly we say that a vector field

X(U):=\Big(\begin{smallmatrix}X(U)^{+}\\
X(U)^{-}\end{smallmatrix}\Big)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{is real-to-real if}\hskip 10.00002pt\overline{X(U)^{+}}=X(U)^{-}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\,U=\Big(\begin{smallmatrix}u\\
\overline{u}\end{smallmatrix}\Big)\,.

If
{\mathcal{U}}:=(U_{1},\ldots,U_{p})
is a
p
-tuple of functions,
\vec{n}:=(n_{1},\cdots,n_{p})\in\mathbb{N}^{p}
, we set

\Pi_{\vec{n}}{\mathcal{U}}:=(\Pi_{n_{1}}U_{1},\cdots,\Pi_{n_{p}}U_{p})\,,\hskip 10.00002pt(\Pi_{n}u)(x):=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}u_{n}e^{{\rm i}nx}+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}u_{-n}e^{-{\rm i}nx}\,.

Paradifferential operators.

We introduce first
paradifferential operators (Definition
A.3
) and then
smoothing operators (Definition
A.4
).

Classes of symbols.

In the definition below we introduce two classes of symbols
{\widetilde{\Gamma}}_{p}^{m}
and
\Gamma_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{m}
. Roughly speaking the class
{\widetilde{\Gamma}}_{p}^{m}
contains symbols of order
m
and homogeneity
p
in
U
, whereas the class
\Gamma_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{m}
contains non-homogeneous symbols of order
m
that vanishes at degree at least
p
in
U
and that are
(K-K^{\prime})
-times differentiable in
t
; we can think of the parameter
K^{\prime}
like the number of time derivatives of
U
that are contained in the symbols.
In the following we denote
\dot{H}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2}):=\bigcap_{s\in\mathbb{R}}\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
.

Definition A.1
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{R}
,
p,{\mathtt{N}}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

K,K^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
K^{\prime}\leq K
, and
r>0
.

(
i
)
p
-
homogeneous symbols.
We denote by
{\widetilde{\Gamma}}^{m}_{p}
the space of symmetric
p
-linear maps
from
(\dot{H}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2}))^{p}
to the space of
C^{\infty}
functions from
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
to
\mathbb{C}
,

(x,\xi)\mapsto a(U_{1},\ldots,U_{p};x,\xi)
, satisfying the following: there exist
\mu>0
and, for any
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
there is a constant
C>0
such that

|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}a(\Pi_{\vec{n}}{\mathcal{U}};x,\xi)|\leq C|\vec{n}|^{\mu+\alpha}\langle\xi\rangle^{m-\beta}\prod_{j=1}^{p}\|\Pi_{n_{j}}U\|_{L^{2}}

(A.3)

for any
{\mathcal{U}}=(U_{1},\dots,U_{p})\in(\dot{H}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2}))^{p}
and
\vec{n}=(n_{1},\dots,n_{p})\in\mathbb{N}^{p}
.
In addition we require the translation invariance property

a\left(\tau_{\varsigma}{\mathcal{U}};x,\xi\right)=a\left({\mathcal{U}};x+\varsigma,\xi\right)
, for any
\varsigma\in\mathbb{R}
,
where
\tau_{\varsigma}
is the translation operator in (
1.10
).
For
p=0
we denote by
{\widetilde{\Gamma}}^{m}_{0}
the space of constant coefficients symbols
\xi\mapsto a(\xi)
which satisfy (
A.3
) with
\alpha=0
and the right hand side replaced by
C\langle\xi\rangle^{m-\beta}
.

We denote by
\sum\limits_{p}^{\mathtt{N}}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{m}_{q}
the class of pluri-homogeneous symbols
\sum_{q=p}^{{\mathtt{N}}}a_{q}
with
a_{q}\in\widetilde{\Gamma}_{q}^{m}
.

(
ii
)
Non-homogeneous symbols.
We denote by
\Gamma_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{m}[r]
the space of functions
(U;t,x,\xi)\mapsto a(U;t,x,\xi)
,
defined for
U\in B_{s_{0}}^{K^{\prime}}(I;r)
for some
s_{0}
large enough, with complex values, such that for any
0\leq k\leq K-K^{\prime}
, any
{\mathtt{s}}\geq s_{0}
, there are
C>0
,
0<r({\mathtt{s}})<r
and for any
U\in B_{s_{0}}^{K}(I;r({\mathtt{s}}))\cap C_{*}^{k+K^{\prime}}(I,\dot{H}^{{\mathtt{s}}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2}))
and any
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, with
\alpha\leq{\mathtt{s}}-s_{0}
one has the estimate

|\partial_{t}^{k}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}a(U;t,x,\xi)|\leq C\langle\xi\rangle^{m-\beta}\|U\|_{k+K^{\prime},s_{0}}^{p-1}\|U\|_{k+K^{\prime},{\mathtt{s}}}\,.

If
p=0
the right hand side has to be replaced by
C\langle\xi\rangle^{m-\beta}
.

(
iii
)
Symbols.
We denote by
\Sigma\Gamma_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{m}[r,{\mathtt{N}}]
the space of functions

(U;t,x,\xi)\mapsto a(U;t,x,\xi),

with complex values such that there are homogeneous symbols
a_{q}\in{\widetilde{\Gamma}}_{q}^{m}
,
q=p,\dots,{\mathtt{N}}
and a non-homogeneous symbol
a_{>{\mathtt{N}}}\in\Gamma_{K,K^{\prime},{\mathtt{N}}+1}^{m}
such that

a(U;t,x,\xi)={\mathop{\sum}}_{q=p}^{{\mathtt{N}}}a_{q}(U,\dots,U;x,\xi)+a_{>{\mathtt{N}}}(U;t,x,\xi)\,.

(A.4)

We denote by
\Sigma\Gamma_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{m}[r,{\mathtt{N}}]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
the space of
2\times 2
matrices with entries in
\Sigma\Gamma_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{m}[r,{\mathtt{N}}]
.

We say that a symbol
a(U;t,x,\xi)
is
real
if it is real valued for any

U\in B^{K^{\prime}}_{s_{0},\mathbb{R}}(I;r)
.

We also define classes of functions in analogy with our classes of symbols.

Definition A.2
.

(Functions)
Let
p,{\mathtt{N}}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

K,K^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
K^{\prime}\leq K
,
r>0
.
We denote by
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{p}
, resp.
\mathcal{F}_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{\mathbb{R}}[r]
,

\Sigma\mathcal{F}_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{\mathbb{R}}[r,{\mathtt{N}}]
,
the subspace of
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{0}_{p}
, resp.
\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[r]
,
resp.
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[r,{\mathtt{N}}]
,
made of those symbols which are independent of
\xi
and
real valued for any
U\in B^{K^{\prime}}_{s_{0},\mathbb{R}}(I;r)
.

Paradifferential quantization.

Given
p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we consider functions

\chi_{p}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
and
\chi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
,
even with respect to each of their arguments, satisfying, for
0<\delta\ll 1
,

\displaystyle{\rm{supp}}\,\chi_{p}\subset\{(\xi^{\prime},\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R};|\xi^{\prime}|\leq\delta\langle\xi\rangle\}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\chi_{p}(\xi^{\prime},\xi)\equiv 1\,\,\,\rm{for}\,\,\,|\xi^{\prime}|\leq\delta\langle\xi\rangle/2\,,

\displaystyle\rm{supp}\,\chi\subset\{(\xi^{\prime},\xi)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R};|\xi^{\prime}|\leq\delta\langle\xi\rangle\}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 10.00002pt\chi(\xi^{\prime},\xi)\equiv 1\,\,\,\rm{for}\,\,\,|\xi^{\prime}|\leq\delta\langle\xi\rangle/2\,.

For
p=0
we set
\chi_{0}\equiv 1
.
We assume moreover that

|\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi^{\prime}}^{\beta}\chi_{p}(\xi^{\prime},\xi)|\leq C_{\alpha,\beta}\langle\xi\rangle^{-\alpha-|\beta|}\,,\ \forall\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{p}\,,\ \ |\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi^{\prime}}^{\beta}\chi(\xi^{\prime},\xi)|\leq C_{\alpha,\beta}\langle\xi\rangle^{-\alpha-\beta},\ \forall\alpha,\,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\,.

The Weyl quantization of a smooth symbol
a(x,\xi)

is the operator
acting on a

2\pi
-periodic function

u(x)

as

{\rm Op}^{W}(a)u=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\hat{a}\big(k-j,\frac{k+j}{2}\big)\hat{u}(j)\Big)\frac{e^{{\rm i}kx}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}

where
\hat{a}(k,\xi)
is the
k^{th}-
Fourier coefficient of the
2\pi-
periodic function
x\mapsto a(x,\xi)
.

Definition A.3
.

(Bony-Weyl quantization)

If
a
is a symbol in
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{m}_{p}
,
respectively in
\Gamma^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[r]
,
we set

a_{\chi_{p}}(\mathcal{U};x,\xi):=\sum_{\vec{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{p}}\chi_{p}\left(\vec{n},\xi\right)a(\Pi_{\vec{n}}\mathcal{U};x,\xi)\,,\hskip 10.00002pta_{\chi}(U;t,x,\xi):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi(\xi^{\prime},\xi)\hat{a}(U;t,\xi^{\prime},\xi)e^{{\rm i}\xi^{\prime}x}{\rm d}\xi^{\prime}\,,

where in the last equality
\hat{a}
stands for the Fourier transform with respect to the
x
variable, and
we define the
Bony-Weyl
quantization of
a
as

{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(a(\mathcal{U};\cdot)):={\rm Op}^{W}(a_{\chi_{p}}(\mathcal{U};\cdot))\,,\hskip 20.00003pt{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(a(U;t,\cdot)):={\rm Op}^{W}(a_{\chi}(U;t,\cdot))\,.

If
a
is a symbol in
\Sigma\Gamma^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[r,{\mathtt{N}}]
,
we define its
Bony-Weyl
quantization

{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(a(U;t,\cdot))={\mathop{\sum}}_{q=p}^{{\mathtt{N}}}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(a_{q}(U,\ldots,U;\cdot))+{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(a_{>{\mathtt{N}}}(U;t,\cdot))\,.

\bullet

The action of

{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(a)
on homogeneous spaces only depends
on the values of the symbol
a=a(U;t,x,\xi)
for
|\xi|\geq 1
. Whenever
we encounter a symbol that is not smooth at
\xi=0
,
such as, for example,
a=g(x)|\xi|^{m}
for
m\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, or
{\textup{sign}}(\xi)
,
we will consider its smoothed out version

\chi(\xi)a
, where

\chi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
is an even and positive cut-off function satisfying

\chi(\xi)=0\;\;{\rm if}\;\;|\xi|\leq\tfrac{1}{8}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\chi(\xi)=1\;\;{\rm if}\;\;|\xi|>\tfrac{1}{4},\hskip 10.00002pt{\partial}_{\xi}\chi(\xi)>0\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\xi\in\big(\tfrac{1}{8},\tfrac{1}{4}\big)\,.

(A.5)

\bullet

A matrix of paradifferential operators
{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A(U;t,x,\xi))
is real-to-real, i.e. (
A.2
) holds, if and only if
the matrix of symbols
A(U;t,x,\xi)
has the form

A(U;x,\xi)=\left(\begin{matrix}{a}(U;t,x,\xi)&{b}(U;t,x,\xi)\\
{\overline{b(U;t,x,-\xi)}}&{\overline{a(U;t,x,-\xi)}}\end{matrix}\right)\,.

We now define smoothing operators.
Roughly speaking, the class
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho}_{p}
contains smoothing operators
which gain
\varrho
derivatives and are homogeneous of degree
p
in
U
, while

\mathcal{R}_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{-\varrho}
contains non-homogeneous
\varrho
-smoothing operators which
vanish at degree at least
p
in
U
, satisfy tame estimates and are
(K-K^{\prime})
-times differentiable in
t
.
The constant
\mu
in (
A.6
)
takes into account losses of derivatives in the “low” frequencies.

Definition A.4
.

(Classes of smoothing operators)

Let
\varrho\geq 0
,
p,{\mathtt{N}}\in\mathbb{N}
,
K,K^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
with
K^{\prime}\leq K
, and
r>0
.

(i)
p
-homogeneous smoothing operators.

We denote by
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho}_{p}

the space of
(p+1)
-linear operators
M

from
(\dot{H}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2}))^{p}\times\dot{H}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C})
to

\dot{H}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C})
which are symmetric
in
(U_{1},\ldots,U_{p})
, of the form

(U_{1},\ldots,U_{p+1})\to R(U_{1},\ldots,U_{p})U_{p+1}

that satisfy the following. There are
\mu\geq 0
and
C>0
such that

\|\Pi_{n_{0}}R(\Pi_{\vec{n}}\mathcal{U})\Pi_{n_{p+1}}U_{p+1}\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\frac{{\rm\max}_{2}(n_{1},\ldots,n_{p+1})^{\varrho+\mu}}{\max(n_{1},\ldots,n_{p+1})^{\varrho}}\prod_{j=1}^{p+1}\|\Pi_{n_{j}}U_{j}\|_{L^{2}}

(A.6)

for any
\mathcal{U}=(U_{1},\ldots,U_{p})\in(\dot{H}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2}))^{p}
, any

U_{p+1}\in\dot{H}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C})
,

\vec{n}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{p})
in
\mathbb{N}^{p}
, any
n_{0},n_{p+1}\in\mathbb{N}
, and where
\max_{2}
denotes the second largest element among
n_{1},\ldots,n_{p+1}
.
In addition we require the translation invariance property

R(\tau_{\varsigma}{\cal U})[\tau_{\varsigma}U_{p+1}]=\tau_{\varsigma}\big(R({\cal U})U_{p+1}\big)
for any

\varsigma\in\mathbb{R}
.

(ii)
Non-homogeneous smoothing-operators.

We denote by
\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[r]

the space of operators
(U,t,V)\mapsto R(U;t)V
defined on
B^{K^{\prime}}_{s_{0}}(I;r)\times I\times C^{0}_{*}(I,\dot{H}^{s_{0}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}))
for some
s_{0}>0
,
which are linear in the variable
V
and such that the following holds true.
For any
s\geq s_{0}
there are
C>0
and

r(s)\in]0,r[
such that for any

U\in B^{K}_{s_{0}}(I;r(s))\cap C^{K}_{*}(I,\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2}))
,
any
V\in C^{K-K^{\prime}}_{*}(I,\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}))
, any
0\leq k\leq K-K^{\prime}
,
t\in I
, we have that

\|{\partial_{t}^{k}\left(R(U;t)V\right)}\|_{\dot{H}^{s-k+\varrho}}\leq C\!\!\!\!\sum_{k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}=k}\!\!\!\!\|{V}\|_{k^{\prime\prime},s}\|{U}\|_{k^{\prime}+K^{\prime},s_{0}}^{p}+\|{V}\|_{k^{\prime\prime},s_{0}}\|U\|_{k^{\prime}+K^{\prime},s_{0}}^{p-1}\|{U}\|_{k^{\prime}+K^{\prime},s}\,.

(A.7)

In case
p=0
we require the estimate

\|{\partial_{t}^{k}\left(R(U;t)V\right)}\|_{\dot{H}^{s-k+\varrho}}\leq C\|{V}\|_{k,s}
.

(iii)
Smoothing operators.

We denote by
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[r,{\mathtt{N}}]
,
the space of operators
(U,t,V)\to R(U;t)V
such that there are homogeneous smoothing operators
R_{q}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{m}_{q}
,
q=p,\ldots,{\mathtt{N}}
and a non–homogeneous smoothing operator
R_{>{\mathtt{N}}}
in

\mathcal{R}^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},{\mathtt{N}}+1}[r]
such that

R(U;t)V={\mathop{\sum}}_{q=p}^{{\mathtt{N}}}R_{q}(U,\ldots,U)V+R_{>{\mathtt{N}}}(U;t)V\,.

We denote by
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[r,{\mathtt{N}}]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

the space of
2\times 2
matrices whose entries are in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[r,\!{\mathtt{N}}]
.

If
R(U_{1},\dots,U_{p})
is a
p
–homogeneous

\varrho
-smoothing operator in
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{p}^{-\varrho}
then
for any
K\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
there exists
s_{0}>0
such that for any
s\geq s_{0}
,
for any
U\in C^{K}_{*}(I,\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2}))
, any
v\in C^{K}_{*}(I,\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}))
, one has (cf. e.g.
[
7
]
[Lemma 2.8])

\|R(U_{1},\dots,U_{p})v\|_{\dot{H}^{s+\varrho}}\lesssim_{K}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\prod_{a=1}^{p}\left\lVert U_{a}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}}}+\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}}}\sum_{\overline{a}=1}^{p}\left\lVert U_{\overline{a}}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\prod_{a=1\atop a\neq\overline{a}}^{p}\left\lVert U_{a}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}}}\,.

(A.8)

To simplify notation we will omit the dependence on the time
t
from the symbols and smoothing remainders. Furthermore
we fix
{\mathtt{N}}=3
and
we write
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,K^{\prime},p}
instead of

\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[r,3]
, and similarly for
functions and smoothing operators.

A.2
Paralinearization of the equations and strategy

We now start proving
2.4
.
The starting point is to write the water waves system
(
1.5
)
in paradifferential form. Precisely
[
5
, Proposition 3.3]
guarantees that for any
K\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varrho\gg 1
, there exists
s_{0}>0
such that for any
s\geq s_{0}
, for any
0<r\leq r_{0}(s)
small enough, if
the variable

U=\begin{pmatrix}u\\
\overline{u}\end{pmatrix}
with
u
defined in (
2.9
) belongs to
B^{K}_{s,\mathbb{R}}(I;r)
(with
s\rightsquigarrow N
and recall notation (
A.1
)) and solves

{\partial}_{t}U={\rm Op}^{BW}\!\left({\rm i}A_{1}(U;x)\xi+{\rm i}A_{\frac{1}{2}}(U;x)|\xi|^{\frac{1}{2}}+A_{0}(U;x)+A_{-1}(U;x,\xi)\right)U+R(U)U

(A.9)

where

\displaystyle A_{1}(U;x):=\begin{pmatrix}-V(U;x)&0\\
0&-V(U;x)\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(A.10)

\displaystyle A_{\frac{1}{2}}(U;x):=\begin{pmatrix}-(1+a(U;x))&-a(U;x)\\
a(U;x)&1+a(U;x)\end{pmatrix}\ ,\hskip 10.00002pta:=\frac{1}{2}\left({\partial}_{t}B+VB_{x}\right)\in\Sigma{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,1,1}\ ,

(A.11)

\displaystyle A_{0}(U;x):=-\frac{1}{4}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}V_{x}(U;x)\ ,

(A.12)

the functions
V(U;x),B(U;x)
belong to

\Sigma{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,1}

(they are the functions (
2.4
)-(
2.5
) expressed in terms of the complex variable
U
),

A_{-1}(U;\cdot)
is a real-to-real matrix of symbols in
\Sigma\Gamma^{-1}_{K,1,1}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and
R(U)

is a real-to-real matrix of smoothing operators
belonging to
\Sigma{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho}_{K,1,1}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

We now explain the main changes we address
with respect to
[
5
]

to achieve
the Lipschitz properties (
2.14
) and (
2.20
) of the normal form transformation.
In order to obtain energy estimates, following
[
5
]
we
block-diagonalize the matrix of symbols in (
A.9
) at positive orders at
any
degree of homogeneity.
Since the matrix
A_{1}(U;x)\xi
in (
A.10
) is already diagonal, one needs to block-diagonalize only the
matrix
A_{\frac{1}{2}}(U;x)|\xi|^{\frac{1}{2}}
in (
A.11
).
This is achieved, as in
[
5
]
, conjugating (
A.9
) with the flow in (
A.27
) generated by a paradifferential operator with a inhomogeneous symbol

m_{-1}(U;x)
in (
A.26
). The first difference is that

\bullet\ (i)

in
A.8
we additionally
prove that the map

U\mapsto m_{-1}(U;x)
is Lipschitz.
We exploit that
m_{-1}(U;x)
is a function of the symbol
a(U;x)
defined in (
A.11
), which we write in terms of
(\eta,\psi)
by computing

{\partial}_{t}B={\rm d}_{\eta,\psi}B(\eta,\psi)[X(\eta,\psi)]={\rm d}_{\eta}B(\eta,\psi)[X^{(\eta)}(\eta,\psi)]+{\rm d}_{\psi}B(\eta,\psi)[X^{(\psi)}(\eta,\psi)]

(A.13)

where
X(\eta,\psi)
is the
water waves vector field in (
1.5
), which is analytic by
(
1.12
), as well as
B
is (
2.7
). In this way we check the Lipschitzianity of

a(U;\cdot)
in
U
and thus of
m_{-1}(U;\cdot)
.

\bullet\ (ii)
as in
[
5
]
we also need to remove the off-diagonal symbols up to very large negative orders (in order to prove energy estimates).
In
[
5
]
these symbols are removed at
any
order of homogeneity. In this way one loses control of the Lipschitz property since the symbols in
A_{-1}(U;x)
are not explicit at any degree (and it is not proved they are Lipschitz).
We overcome this issue by removing those symbols only up to cubic degree of homogeneity. As a result,
the normal form transformations we perform are
the flow in (
A.38
) generated
by the matrix of symbols
M^{(j)}(U)
in (
A.39
) which are pluri-homogeneous in
U
,
thus satisfy a Lipschitz property in
U
, as we show in the
next section.

Notation:
In the sequence
s_{0}>0
denotes the index of a low Sobolev norm, that may change from line to line.

A.3
Lipschitz properties of linear flows

We consider linear flows of the form

\begin{cases}{\partial}_{\tau}\Phi^{\tau}(U)=G(\tau,U)\Phi^{\tau}(U)\\
\Phi^{0}(U)={\rm Id}\end{cases}

(A.14)

where
G(\tau,U)
is a matrix of real-to-real paradifferential or smoothing operators.
Similarly to
[
54
]
, we prove that
the flow map
U\mapsto\Phi^{\tau}(U)

is Lipschitz provided

U\mapsto G(\tau,U)
is (in suitable topologies).
We first
consider the case
G(\tau,U)
is a paradifferential operator.

Lemma A.5
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}
,
{\mathtt{N}}
with
p\leq{\mathtt{N}}
,
\tau\in[0,1]
.
Let
G(\tau,U)
be either

(i)

{\rm Op}^{BW}\!\left({\Big[\begin{smallmatrix}\frac{\beta(U;x)}{1+\tau\beta_{x}(U;x)}{\rm i}\xi&0\\
0&\frac{\beta(U;x)}{1+\tau\beta_{x}(U;x)}{\rm i}\xi\end{smallmatrix}\Big]}\right)
with
\beta(U;x)
a
p
-homogeneous real value function in
{\widetilde{{\mathcal{F}}}}_{p}^{\mathbb{R}}
;

(ii)

{\rm Op}^{BW}\!\left({\Big[\begin{smallmatrix}{\rm i}f(U;x,\xi)&0\\
0&-{\rm i}\overline{f(U;x,-\xi)}\end{smallmatrix}\Big]}\right)
with
f(U;x,\xi)
a
p
-homogeneous symbol in
{\widetilde{\Gamma}}_{p}^{m},m<1
, which additionally is real valued in case
m\in(0,1)
;

(iii)

{\rm Op}^{BW}\!\left({\Big[\begin{smallmatrix}0&g(U;x,\xi)\\
\overline{g(U;x,-\xi)}&0\end{smallmatrix}\Big]}\right)
with
g(U;x,\xi)
a pluri-homogeneous symbol in
\sum_{p}^{\mathtt{N}}{\widetilde{\Gamma}}^{m}_{q}
,
m\leq 0
, or
a function
g(U;x)
satisfying
g(0;\cdot)=0
and the Lipschitz estimate

\left\lVert g(U_{1};x)-g(U_{2};x)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\left\lVert U_{1}-U_{2}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}}}\,.

(A.15)

Then, there exists
s_{0},r>0
such that, for any
U\in\dot{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{s_{0}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
satisfying

\left\lVert U\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}}}\leq r
, the flow
\Phi^{\tau}(U)
in (
A.14
) is well defined,
\Phi^{\tau}(0)={\rm Id}
, and satisfies
for any
s\in\mathbb{R}

and
V\in\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
the bound

\sup_{\tau\in[0,1]}\left\lVert\Phi^{\tau}(U)V\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq(1+C_{s}\left\lVert U\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}}})\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\,,

(A.16)

as well as its inverse
\Phi^{\tau}(U)^{-1}
. In addition

Lipschitz property:

There exist
s_{0},r>0
such that
for any
U_{j}\in\dot{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{s_{0}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
with
\left\lVert U_{j}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}}}\leq r
,
j=1,2
,
any
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
any
Z\in\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
,

\sup_{\tau\in[0,1]}\left\lVert\Phi^{\tau}(U_{1})Z-\Phi^{\tau}(U_{2})Z\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s-m}}\leq C_{s,r}\left\lVert U_{1}-U_{2}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}}}\left\lVert Z\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}

(A.17)

for some
C_{s,r}>0
, and with

m=1
in case of item
(i)
.

Proof.

Under the assumption
(i)
or
(ii)
or
(iii)
, the flow
\Phi^{\tau}(U)
is well defined for any
\tau\in[0,1]
and
satisfies (
A.16
) by
[
7
, Lemma 3.16
(i)
]
.
Let us prove the Lipschitz estimate (
A.17
).
For any
U_{j}\in\dot{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{s_{0}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
,
j=1,2
, with
\left\lVert U_{j}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}}}\leq r
, the difference

\Phi^{\vartheta}(U_{1})-\Phi^{\vartheta}(U_{2})

fulfills the variational equation

\begin{cases}\partial_{\vartheta}\Big(\Phi^{\vartheta}(U_{1})-\Phi^{\vartheta}(U_{2})\Big)=G(\vartheta,U_{2})\Big(\Phi^{\vartheta}(U_{1})-\Phi^{\vartheta}(U_{2})\Big)+\Big(G(\vartheta,U_{1})-G(\vartheta,U_{2})\Big)\Phi^{\vartheta}(U_{1})\\
\Phi^{0}(U_{1})-\Phi^{0}(U_{2})=0\,,\end{cases}

whose solution is given by the Duhamel formula

\displaystyle\Phi^{\vartheta}(U_{1})-\Phi^{\vartheta}(U_{2})=\Phi^{\vartheta}(U_{2})\int_{0}^{\vartheta}\Phi^{\tau}(U_{2})^{-1}\ \Big(G(\tau,U_{1})-G(\tau,U_{2})\Big)\ \Phi^{\tau}(U_{1})\,{\rm d}\tau\ .

(A.18)

We claim that, for
G(\tau,U)
as in item
(i),(ii)
or
(iii)
, for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, any
Z\in\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
,

\sup_{\tau\in[0,1]}\left\lVert\Big(G(\tau,U_{1})-G(\tau,U_{2})\Big)Z\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s-m}}\leq C_{s,r}\left\lVert U_{1}-U_{2}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}}}\,\left\lVert Z\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}},

(A.19)

where
m=1
in case
(i)
.
Then the Lipschitz estimate (
A.17
) follows
by (
A.18
), (
A.19
) and (
A.16
).

It remains to prove (
A.19
) which
is a consequence of the estimate (
2.3
) for the action of
paradifferential operators.
In case
(i)
, we have

\Big(G(\tau,U_{1})-G(\tau,U_{2})\Big)Z={\rm Op}^{BW}\!\left({\Big[\begin{smallmatrix}a(\tau;x,\xi)&0\\
0&a(\tau;x,\xi)\end{smallmatrix}\Big]}\right)Z\

(A.20)

where

\displaystyle a(\tau;x,\xi)

\displaystyle:=\left(\frac{\beta(U_{1};x)}{1+\tau\beta_{x}(U_{1};x)}-\frac{\beta(U_{2};x)}{1+\tau\beta_{x}(U_{2};x)}\right){\rm i}\xi

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta(U_{1};x)-\beta(U_{2};x)}{1+\tau\beta_{x}(U_{1};x)}{\rm i}\xi-\tau\frac{\beta(U_{2};x)\left(\beta_{x}(U_{1};x)-\beta_{x}(U_{2};x)\right)}{(1+\tau\beta_{x}(U_{1};x))\,(1+\tau\beta_{x}(U_{2};x))}{\rm i}\xi\,.

Since
\beta(U;x)\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{F}}}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{p}
is a homogeneous polynomial in
U
, one has

\beta(U_{1};x)-\beta(U_{2};x)=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}\leq\cdots\leq i_{p-1}\leq 2}\beta(U_{1}-U_{2},U_{i_{1}},\cdots,U_{i_{p-1}};x)

implying, by the very definition (
A.3
) (for a
function so
m\rightsquigarrow 0
,
\beta\rightsquigarrow 0
), the estimate

\left\lVert\beta(U_{1};\cdot)-\beta(U_{2};\cdot)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+\left\lVert\beta_{x}(U_{1};\cdot)-\beta_{x}(U_{2};\cdot)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C_{r}\left\lVert U_{1}-U_{2}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}}}

for some
C_{r}>0
.
Then the seminorm (
2.1
) of the symbol
a(\tau;x,\xi)
is bounded by

\sup_{\tau\in[0,1]}|a(\tau;\cdot)|_{1,L^{\infty},4}=\max_{|\beta|\leq 4}\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\big\|\langle\xi\rangle^{-1+|\beta|}\,{\partial}_{\xi}^{\beta}a(\tau;\cdot,\xi)\big\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C_{r}\left\lVert U_{1}-U_{2}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}}}

and (
2.3
) implies that the operator (
A.20
) satisfies (
A.19
).

Similar arguments prove estimate (
A.19
) also in cases
(ii)
,
(iii)
.
∎

Now we consider the case
G(\tau,U)
is a matrix of smoothing operators.

Lemma A.6
.

Let
\varrho\geq 0
,
p\in\mathbb{N}
and

G(\tau,U):=R(U)
be a matrix of smothing operators in
{\widetilde{{\mathcal{R}}}}^{-\varrho}_{p}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
Then there is
s_{0}\geq 0
such that for any
s\geq s_{0}
, there is
r=r(s),C_{s,r}>0
so that for
any
\left\lVert U\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq r
, the flow
\Phi^{\tau}(U)
in (
A.14
)
is a well defined bounded operator in

\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
, satisfies

\Phi^{\tau}(0)={\rm Id}
, and

\left\lVert[\Phi^{\tau}(U)]^{\pm 1}V\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq C_{s}\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}(1+C_{s}\left\lVert U\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}}})+C_{s}\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}}}\left\lVert U\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}

(A.21)

uniformly in
\tau\in[0,1]
. In addition one has

Lipschitz property:
for
any
\left\lVert U_{i}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq r
,
i=1,2
, any
Z\in\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
, one has

\left\lVert\Phi^{\tau}(U_{1})Z-\Phi^{\tau}(U_{2})Z\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq C_{s,r}\left\lVert U_{1}-U_{2}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\left\lVert Z\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\ .

(A.22)

Proof.

The well-definition of the flow
\Phi^{\tau}(U)
and (
A.21
) follow from

[
5
, Lemma A.3]
with
K=0
.
The proof of (
A.22
) follows
similarly to the previous lemma. Formula (
A.18
) holds with
G(\tau,U_{1})-G(\tau,U_{2})
replaced by
R(U_{1})-R(U_{2})
.
Then replace
estimate (
A.19
) by

\left\lVert R(U_{1})Z-R(U_{2})Z\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{1\leq i_{1}\leq\cdots\leq i_{p-1}\leq 2}\left\lVert R(U_{1}-U_{2},U_{i_{1}},\cdots,U_{i_{p-1}})Z\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq C_{s,r}\left\lVert U_{1}-U_{2}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\left\lVert Z\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}

obtained applying (
A.8
).
∎

A.4
Normal form with a Lipschitz map

We now start the normal form procedure.

Block-diagonalization up to quartic degree bounded terms.

The first step is to block-diagonalize system (
A.9
) in the variables
(u,\overline{u})
up to a quartic degree term.
The following result is a variant of
[
5
, Proposition 3.10]
with a new bounded remainder
B(U)W
in (
A.23
) which satisfies the quartic bound (
A.25
).

Proposition A.7
.

Let
\varrho\gg 1
and
K\geq K^{\prime}:=2\varrho+2
. There exists
s_{0}>0
such that,
for any
s\geq s_{0}
, for all
0<r\leq r_{0}(s)
small enough,
and any solution
U\in B^{K}_{s,\mathbb{R}}(I;r)
of (
A.9
), the following holds:

(i)

there is a map

\Psi_{diag}^{\theta}(U)
,
\theta\in[0,1]
, satisfying, for some
C=C(s,r,K)>0
, for any

V\in\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})

\|\Psi_{diag}^{\theta}(U)V\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}+\|(\Psi_{diag}^{\theta}(U))^{-1}V\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq\big(1+C\|{U}\|_{K,s_{0}}\big)\|V\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}\,;

(ii)

the function
W:=(\Psi_{diag}^{\theta}(U)U)_{|_{\theta=1}}
solves the real-to-real system

{\partial}_{t}W={{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\left({\Big[\begin{smallmatrix}d(U;x,\xi)+r_{-1/2}(U;x,\xi)&0\\
0&\overline{d(U;x,-\xi)}+\overline{r_{-1/2}(U;x,-\xi)}\end{smallmatrix}\Big]}\right)W+R(U)W+B(U)W

(A.23)

where
d(U;x,\xi)
is a symbol of the form

d(U;x,\xi):=-{\rm i}V(U;x)\xi-{\rm i}(1+a^{(0)}(U;x))|\xi|^{1/2}

(A.24)

where

a^{(0)}(U;x)
is a function in
\Sigma{{\mathcal{F}}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,1,1}
,

r_{-1/2}(U;x,\xi)
is a symbol in
\Sigma{\Gamma}^{-1/2}_{K,2\varrho+2,1}
,

R(U)
is a real-to-real matrix of smoothing operators in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,2\varrho+2,1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
,
and
B(U)
satisfies the estimate for any
s\in\mathbb{R}

\left\lVert B(U)V\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq C_{s}\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\left\lVert U\right\rVert_{2\varrho+1,s_{0}}^{3}\,.

(A.25)

Lipschitz property:
The operator
\Psi_{diag}^{\theta}(U)
satisfies a Lipschitz property as
(
A.17
) and
\Psi_{diag}^{\theta}(0)={\rm Id}
.

Proposition
A.7
is proved by
applying a sequence a transformations
which iteratively block-diagonalize (
A.9
) in decreasing orders up to the quartic-degree bounded term
B(U)W
.

Step 1: Block diagonalization at order
\frac{1}{2}
.

We diagonalize the matrix
of symbols
A_{\frac{1}{2}}(U;x)|\xi|^{\frac{1}{2}}

in (
A.9
), up to a matrix of symbols of order 0.
The following lemma is
[
5
, Lemma 3.11]
, and we prove in addition that
the normal form map satisfies a Lipschitz property as
(
A.17
).

Lemma A.8
.

Define the function

\displaystyle m_{-1}(U;x):=-\log\Big(\frac{1+\lambda}{\sqrt{(1+a+\lambda)^{2}-a^{2}}}\Big)\in\Sigma\mathcal{F}_{K,1,1}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\lambda(U;x):=\sqrt{1+2a(U;x)}\,,

(A.26)

where

a(U;x)
is the function in (
A.11
), and
the flow

{\partial}_{\theta}\Psi_{-1}^{\theta}(U)={{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(M_{-1})\Psi_{-1}^{\theta}(U),\hskip 10.00002pt\Psi_{-1}^{0}(U)={\rm Id}\,,\hskip 10.00002ptM_{-1}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&m_{-1}(U;x)\\
\overline{m_{-1}(U;x)}&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(A.27)

If
U
solves (
A.9
), then the function

W_{0}:=(\Psi_{-1}^{\theta}(U))_{|_{\theta=1}}U

(A.28)

solves the real-to-real system

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}W_{0}={{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\Big({\Big[\begin{smallmatrix}d(U;x,\xi)&0\\
0&\overline{d(U;x,-\xi)}\end{smallmatrix}\Big]}+A^{(0)}\Big)W_{0}+R^{(0)}(U)W_{0}

(A.29)

where
d(U;x,\xi)
is the symbol in (
A.24
) with

a^{(0)}(U;x):=\lambda(U;x)-1\in\Sigma{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,1,1}
,
a matrix of symbols

\displaystyle A^{(0)}:=\left(\begin{matrix}c_{0}(U;x,\xi)&b_{0}(U;x,\xi)\\
\overline{b_{0}(U;x,-\xi)}&\overline{c_{0}(U;x,-\xi)}\end{matrix}\right)\,,\ c_{0}\in\Sigma\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{K,2,1}\,,\;\;b_{0}\in\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,2,1}\,,

(A.30)

and a real-to-real matrix of smoothing operators
R^{(0)}(U)
in

\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,2,1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Lipschitz property:
The operator
\Psi_{-1}^{\theta}(U)
satisfies a Lipschitz property as
(
A.17
) and
\Psi_{-1}^{\theta}(0)={\rm Id}
.

Proof.

We prove only the Lipschitz property of the flow map
\Psi_{-1}^{\theta}(U)
,
the other statements are proved in
[
5
, Lemma 3.11]
. It follows by Lemma
A.5

(iii)
once we prove that
the function
m_{-1}(U;x)
satisfies the
Lipschitz estimate (
A.15
).
We now prove that, provided

{\mathtt{s}}>5
and
r>0
is small enough, the map

B^{{\mathtt{s}}}_{\mathbb{R}}(r):=\{U\in H_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathtt{s}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2}),\|U\|_{{\dot{H}}^{\mathtt{s}}}<r\}\to H^{{\mathtt{s}}-\frac{9}{4}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\,,\hskip 10.00002ptU\mapsto m_{-1}(U;\cdot)\ \ \text{is analytic}\,,

(A.31)

and consequently
m_{-1}(U_{1};\cdot)-m_{-1}(U_{2};\cdot)
satisfies the Lipschitz estimate (
A.15
).
The explicit form of the function
m_{-1}(U;x)
in
(
A.26
) is obtained by a direct
calculus.
Since
m_{-1}(U;x)
depends on
U
only through the function
a(U;x)
, and the map
a\mapsto m_{-1}
is analytic provided
\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{H^{s_{0}}}
is small enough, by composition,
it is sufficient to
prove, for any
{\mathtt{s}}>5
,
the analyticity of the map

B^{{\mathtt{s}}}_{\mathbb{R}}(r)\to H^{{\mathtt{s}}-\frac{9}{4}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\,,\hskip 10.00002ptU\mapsto a(U;\cdot)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{A12}}}{{=}}\tfrac{1}{2}\left({\partial}_{t}B+VB_{x}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:patB}}}{{=}}\tfrac{1}{2}\big({\rm d}_{\eta,\psi}B(\eta,\psi)[X(\eta,\psi)]+VB_{x}\big)

(A.32)

where
X(\eta,\psi)
is the water waves vector field,
which is analytic as stated in (
1.12
), and
V,B
are analytic as well in
(\eta,\psi)
as stated in (
2.7
).
Thus the map

(\eta,\psi)\mapsto a(\eta,\psi)

is analytic from

B^{{\mathtt{s}}}(r)\times\dot{H}^{{\mathtt{s}}}(\mathbb{T})\to H^{{\mathtt{s}}-2}(\mathbb{T})
for any
{\mathtt{s}}>\frac{9}{2}
.
To conclude the proof it is sufficient to
express

(\eta,\psi)
as functions of
U
,
and prove that this mapping is analytic. First note
that the good unknown map
(\eta,\psi)\mapsto{\mathcal{G}}(\eta,\psi)
in (
2.8
)
is, for any
{\mathtt{s}}>\tfrac{7}{2}
locally invertible around zero with an analytic inverse

(\eta,\upomega)\mapsto(\eta,\psi)={\mathcal{G}}^{-1}(\eta,\upomega)
because
{\rm d}{\mathcal{G}}(0,0)={\rm Id}
.
Hence, cf. (
2.9
), (
2.6
),

H^{\sigma}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})\to H^{{\mathtt{s}}-\frac{1}{4}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\times H^{{\mathtt{s}}-\frac{1}{4}}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})\ ,\hskip 10.00002ptU=\Big(\begin{smallmatrix}u\\
\overline{u}\end{smallmatrix}\Big)\mapsto(\eta,\psi)={\mathcal{G}}^{-1}\Big(\frac{|D|^{\frac{1}{4}}[u+\overline{u}]}{\sqrt{2}}\,,\ \frac{|D|^{-\frac{1}{4}}[u-\overline{u}]}{{\rm i}\sqrt{2}}\Big)

is analytic
being composition of analytic maps.
As a result, by composition with the analytic
map in (
A.32
)
we deduce
(
A.31
).
∎

Step 2: Block diagonalization at negative orders.

We now block-diagonalize the matrix of symbols
A^{(0)}
in (
A.30
) using the flow of paradifferential operators with pluri-homogeneous symbols.
Given a symbol
a(U;x,\xi)
in
\Sigma\Gamma_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{m}[r,N]
of the form (
A.4
)
we denote, for
p\leq q\leq{\mathtt{N}}
, the projections on the pluri-homogeneous symbols

{\mathcal{P}}_{\leq q}[a(U;x,\xi)]:=\sum_{l=p}^{q}a_{l}(U;x,\xi)\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt{\mathcal{P}}_{\geq q+1}[a(U;x,\xi)]:=a(U;x,\xi)-{\mathcal{P}}_{\leq q}[a(U;x,\xi)]\,.

Lemma A.9
.

For
j=0,\ldots,2\varrho
, there are
s_{0}>0
and

\bullet

paradifferential operators of the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}^{(j)}(U):={{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\Big({\Big[\begin{smallmatrix}d(U;x,\xi)&0\\
0&\overline{d(U;x,-\xi)}\end{smallmatrix}\Big]}\Big)+{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}(A^{(j)})

(A.33)

where
d(U;x,\xi)
is the symbol defined in (
A.24
),

A^{(j)}
is a matrix of symbols of the form

A^{(j)}=\left(\begin{matrix}c_{j}(U;x,\xi)&b_{j}(U;x,\xi)\\
\overline{b_{j}(U;x,-\xi)}&\overline{c_{j}(U;x,-\xi)}\end{matrix}\right),\;\;c_{j}\in\Sigma\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{K,j+2,1},\;\;b_{j}\in\Sigma\Gamma^{-\frac{j}{2}}_{K,j+2,1}\,,

(A.34)

\bullet

a real-to-real matrix of smoothing operators
R^{(j)}(U)
in

\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,j+2,1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
,

\bullet

a real-to-real matrix of operators
B^{(j)}(U)
satisfying
for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, for any
V\in\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
,

\|B^{(j)}(U)V\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq C_{s}\left\lVert U\right\rVert_{j+1,s_{0}}^{3}\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}

(A.35)

such that, if
W_{j}
,
j=0,\ldots,2\varrho-1
, solves

{\partial}_{t}W_{j}=\big(\mathcal{Y}^{(j)}(U)+R^{(j)}(U)+B^{(j)}(U)\big)W_{j},\hskip 10.00002ptW_{j}:=\begin{pmatrix}w_{j}\\
\overline{w}_{j}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(A.36)

then

W_{j+1}:=(\Psi_{j}^{\theta}(U)W_{j})_{|_{\theta=1}}\,,

(A.37)

where
\Psi_{j}^{\theta}(U)
is the flow at time
\theta\in[0,1]
of

\partial_{\theta}\Psi^{\theta}_{j}(U)={\rm i}{{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}{(M^{(j)}(U;x,\xi))}\Psi_{j}^{\theta}(U)\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\Psi_{j}^{0}(U)={\rm Id}\,,

(A.38)

with

\!\!\small M^{(j)}(U;x,\xi):=\begin{pmatrix}0&-{\rm i}m_{j}(U;x,\xi)\\
-{\rm i}\,\overline{m_{j}(U;x,-\xi)}&0\end{pmatrix}\,,\,\;\;m_{j}:={\mathcal{P}}_{\leq 2}\Big(\frac{-\chi(\xi)b_{j}(U;x,\xi)}{2{\rm i}(1+a^{(0)}(U;x))|\xi|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\Big)\in\sum_{1}^{2}{\widetilde{\Gamma}}^{-\frac{j+1}{2}}_{p}\,,

(A.39)

and
\chi
is defined in (
A.5
),
satisfies a system of the form (
A.36
) with
j+1
instead of
j
.

Lipschitz property:

The operator
\Psi_{j}^{\theta}(U)
satisfies a Lipschitz property as
(
A.17
) and
\Psi_{j}^{\theta}(0)={\rm Id}
.

Proof.

The Lipschitz property of
\Psi_{j}^{\theta}(U)
follows by Lemma
A.5

(iii)
since the symbol
M^{(j)}(U)
in (
A.39
) is pluri-homogeneous.
The rest of the proof proceeds by induction.

Inizialization.
System (
A.29
) is (
A.36
) for
j=0
where the
paradifferential operator
\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(U)
has the form (
A.33
) with the matrix of symbols

A^{(0)}
defined in (
A.30
) and
B^{(0)}=0
.

Iteration.

We argue by induction. Suppose that
W_{j}
solves system (
A.36
) with operators

\mathcal{Y}^{(j)}(U)
of the form (
A.33
)-(
A.34
), smoothing operators

R^{(j)}(U)
in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,j+2,1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and
a matrix of real-to-real operators
B^{(j)}(U)
satisfying
(
A.35
).
By
formula
[
5
, (A.2)]
the conjugated system has the form

{\partial}_{t}W_{j+1}=\big(({\partial}_{t}\Psi_{j}^{1}(U))\Psi_{j}^{-1}(U)+\Psi_{j}^{1}(U)\mathcal{Y}^{(j)}(U)\Psi_{j}^{-1}(U)+\Psi_{j}^{1}(U)B^{(j)}(U)\Psi_{j}^{-1}(U)\big)W_{j+1}

(A.40)

up to a smoothing operator in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,j+2,1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

By the proof of
[
5
, Lemma 3.12]
,

({\partial}_{t}\Psi_{j}^{1}(U))\Psi_{j}^{-1}(U)

is a
paradifferential operator with symbol in
\Sigma\Gamma^{-\frac{j+1}{2}}_{K,j+3,1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
plus a
smoothing operator in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,j+3,1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, and

\displaystyle\Psi_{j}^{1}(U)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}^{(j)}(U)\Psi_{j}^{-1}(U)={{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\Big({\Big[\begin{smallmatrix}d(U;x,\xi)&0\\
0&\overline{d(U;x,-\xi)}\end{smallmatrix}\Big]}+{\Big[\begin{smallmatrix}c_{j}(U;x,\xi)&q_{j}(U;x,\xi)\\
\overline{q_{j}(U;x,-\xi)}&\overline{c_{j}(U;x,-\xi)}\end{smallmatrix}\Big]}\Big)\ ,

(A.41)

\displaystyle q_{j}(U;x,\xi):=\chi(\xi)b_{j}(U;x,\xi)+2{\rm i}m_{j}(U;x,\xi)(1+a^{(0)}(U;x))|\xi|^{\frac{1}{2}}\,,

(A.42)

plus a paradifferential operator with symbol in
\Sigma\Gamma^{-\frac{j+1}{2}}_{K,j+2,1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

and a
smoothing operator belonging to

\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,j+2,1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Consider now the off-diagonal symbol

q_{j}(U;x,\xi)
in (
A.41
).
By (
A.42
) and
the choice of
m_{j}(U;x,\xi)
in (
A.39
) we have that

\displaystyle q_{j}(U;x,\xi)

\displaystyle=(1+a^{(0)}(U;x))\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\geq 3}\left(\frac{\chi(\xi)b_{j}(U;x,\xi)}{1+a^{(0)}(U;x)}\right)\in\Gamma^{0}_{K,j+2,3}[r]\,.

(A.43)

We conclude that (
A.37
) solves a system of the form
(
A.33
)-(
A.36
)
with
j\rightsquigarrow j+1
and

\displaystyle B^{(j+1)}(U)

\displaystyle:={{\rm Op}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}W}}}}\Big({{\Big[\begin{smallmatrix}0&q_{j}(U;x,\xi)\\
\overline{q_{j}(U;x,-\xi)}&0\end{smallmatrix}\Big]}}\Big)+\Psi_{j}^{1}(U)B^{(j)}(U)\Psi_{j}^{-1}(U)

which satisfies, by (
A.43
),
[
3
, Proposition 3.8]
,
\Psi_{j}^{\theta}(U)
satisfies
(
A.16
), (
A.35
), the bound

\|B^{(j+1)}(U)V\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}\lesssim_{s}\left\lVert U\right\rVert_{j+2,s_{0}}^{3}\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}
,
namely (
A.35
) with
j\leadsto j+1
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
A.7
.

The proof follows combining Lemmata
A.8
and
A.9
, defining

\Psi_{diag}^{\theta}(U):=\Psi_{2\varrho-1}^{\theta}(U)\circ\cdots\Psi_{0}^{\theta}(U)\circ\Psi_{-1}^{\theta}(U)

where the maps
\Psi_{j}^{\theta}(U)
,
j=0,\ldots,2\varrho-1
, resp.
\Psi_{-1}^{\theta}(U)
, are defined in (
A.37
), resp. (
A.28
).
Then
\Psi_{diag}^{\theta}(U)
satisfies a Lipschitz property as (
A.17
) since all the maps
\Psi_{j}^{\theta}(U)
,
j=0,\cdots,2\varrho-1
and
\Psi_{-1}^{\theta}(U)
satisfy estimates
(
A.16
) and (
A.17
). Finally
\Psi_{diag}^{\theta}(0)={\rm Id}
as well as all the maps

\Psi_{j}^{\theta}(0)
and
\Psi_{-1}^{\theta}(0)
.
∎

Reductions to constant coefficients and Poincaré-Birkhoff normal form.

At this point the proof is analogous to
[
5
, Propositions 4.4, 5.2, and 6.2]
.

Proposition A.10
.

There exists
\varrho_{0}>0
such that, for all
\varrho\geq\varrho_{0}
,
K\geq K^{\prime}=2\varrho+2
,
there exists
s_{0}>0
such that,
for any
s\geq s_{0}
, for all
0<r\leq r_{0}(s)
small enough,
and any solution
U\in B^{K}_{s}(I;r)
of the water waves system (
A.9
), there is a
real-to-real, bounded and invertible operator

\mathfrak{C}^{\theta}(U)
,

\theta\in[0,1]
,
such that

Z:=\Big(\begin{smallmatrix}z\\
\overline{z}\end{smallmatrix}\Big)=\mathfrak{C}^{1}(U)[U]

solves

{\partial}_{t}Z=-{\rm i}{\bf\Omega}Z+X_{H^{(4)}_{ZD}}+\mathcal{X}_{\geq 4}(U,Z)

(A.44)

where:

•

{\bf\Omega}:=\begin{pmatrix}|D|^{\frac{1}{2}}&0\\
0&-|D|^{\frac{1}{2}}\end{pmatrix}
and
X_{H^{(4)}_{ZD}}
is the Hamiltonian vector field of the Hamiltonian
H_{ZD}^{(4)}
in (
2.17
);

•

\mathcal{X}_{\geq 4}(U,Z)
has the form

\mathcal{X}_{\geq 4}(U,Z)={\rm Op}^{BW}\!\left({\Big[\begin{smallmatrix}{{H}}_{\geq 3}(U;x,\xi)&0\\
0&\overline{{{H}}_{\geq 3}(U;x,-\xi)}\end{smallmatrix}\Big]}\right)Z+\mathfrak{R}_{\geq 3}(U)Z+B_{\geq 3}(U)Z

(A.45)

where

H_{\geq 3}(U;x,\xi):={\rm i}\alpha_{\geq 3}(U;x)\xi+{\rm i}\beta_{\geq 3}(U;x)|\xi|^{\frac{1}{2}}+\gamma_{\geq 3}(U;x,\xi)\in\Gamma^{1}_{K,K^{\prime},3}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

(A.46)

with real valued functions
\alpha_{\geq 3}(U;x),\beta_{\geq 3}(U;x)
in
{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,K^{\prime},3}
and a symbol

\gamma_{\geq 3}(U;x,\xi)
in
\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},3}
,

\mathfrak{R}_{\geq 3}(U)
is a matrix of real-to-real smoothing operators in

\mathcal{R}^{-(\varrho-\varrho_{0})}_{K,K^{\prime},3}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

and
B_{\geq 3}(U)
a matrix of operators satisfying

\left\lVert B_{\geq 3}(U)V\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq C_{s}\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\left\lVert U\right\rVert_{K^{\prime},s_{0}}^{3}+C_{s}\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}}}\left\lVert U\right\rVert_{K^{\prime},s}\left\lVert U\right\rVert_{K^{\prime},s_{0}}^{3}\,.

(A.47)

Furthermore the map
\mathfrak{C}^{\theta}(U)
satisfies the following properties:

(i) there is a constant
C
depending on
s
,
r
and
K
, such that for any
s\geq s_{0}
and any
V\in\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})

\|\mathfrak{C}^{\theta}(U)[V]\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}+\|(\mathfrak{C}^{\theta}(U))^{-1}[V]\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq\|V\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}(1+C\|U\|_{K,s_{0}})+C\|V\|_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}}}\|U\|_{K,s}\,,

(A.48)

uniformly in
\theta\in[0,1]
;

Lipschitz property:

for any
s\geq s_{0}
, there is
r=r(s),C_{s,r}>0
so that for any
\left\lVert U_{i}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq r
,
i=1,2
,
any
V\in\dot{H}^{s+1}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
, one has

\left\lVert\mathfrak{C}^{1}(U_{1})V-\mathfrak{C}^{1}(U_{2})V\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq C_{s}\left\lVert U_{1}-U_{2}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s+1}}\,.

(A.49)

Finally
\mathfrak{C}^{\theta}(0)={\rm Id}
.

Proof.

We only prove that
\mathfrak{C}^{\theta}(U)
satisfies the Lipschitz estimate
(
A.49
) and
that the new term

satisfies (
A.47
).
This requires to analyze the structure of the map
\mathfrak{C}^{\theta}(U)
which is the same as
[
5
]
.

In particular

are time flows

) with

G(\tau,U)={\rm Op}^{BW}\!\left({\Big[\begin{smallmatrix}\frac{\beta(U;x)}{1+\tau\beta_{x}(U;x)}{\rm i}\xi&0\\
0&\frac{\beta(U;x)}{1+\tau\beta_{x}(U;x)}{\rm i}\xi\end{smallmatrix}\Big]}\right)\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\beta\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{F}}}}_{i}^{\mathbb{R}},\ \ i=1,2\,,

,

Proof of Theorem
2.4
.

We define

\mathfrak{B}(u)u:=[\mathfrak{C}^{1}(U)U]^{+}
the
u
-component of the map
\mathfrak{C}^{1}(U)U
where
\mathfrak{C}^{1}(U)
is defined in
A.10
.
Then
z=\mathfrak{B}(u)u
solves (
2.15
) in view of (
A.44
).
Estimate (
2.13
) follows from (
A.48
) and
2.7
.
It implies the equivalence of norms

C_{s}^{-1}\left\lVert z\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leq C_{s}\left\lVert z\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}
for some
C_{s}>0
.
The first estimate in
(
2.14
) follows from the Lipschitz estimate (
A.22
) for
\mathfrak{C}^{1}(U)
putting
U_{2}=0
, and the second estimate follows from the first one writing

\mathfrak{B}(U)^{-1}-{\rm Id}=-\mathfrak{B}(U)^{-1}\circ(\mathfrak{B}(U)-{\rm Id})
.
The vector field
\mathcal{X}_{\geq 4}(U,Y)
is defined in (
A.45
).
Its first two terms satisfy
the energy estimate (
2.18
) by
[
5
, Lemma 6.4]
.
Also the third term
B_{\geq 3}(U)Z
satisfies the same estimate by
(
A.47
), (
2.36
) and the equivalence of norms
\left\lVert z\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}\sim\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s}}
.
Now we prove estimate
(
2.19
).
By
[
3
, Proposition 3.8]
, estimate (
A.7
) for
\mathfrak{R}_{\geq 3}(U)
and (
A.47
) we get

\left\lVert{\mathcal{X}_{\geq 4}^{+}(U,Z)}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1}}\lesssim\left\lVert U\right\rVert_{K,s_{0}}^{3}\left\lVert z\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Germe},\eqref{BISMA2}}}{{\lesssim}}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s_{0}}}^{4}\,.

Estimate (
2.20
) follows because
\mathfrak{C}^{\theta}(U)
satisfies
eqs.

A.49
and
A.48
.
